# Implementation notes -- the FkRecipes build report

Working notes for the v1 build, 2026-08-31. This file is a deliverable equal to the code: every design deviation with evidence, every piece of ecosystem friction met while dogfooding FkLua's distribution machinery, every cross-language mirror gap, and the gate results. Unresolved issues are recorded here, never papered over. The charter is FkLua's agents/drafts/recipes-library-design.md (decisions resolved 2026-08-31).

## Ecosystem friction, graded

Each entry says what was tried, what happened verbatim, and the grade: CLEAN (worked as documented), AWKWARD (worked but cost something), MISLED (pointed the wrong way), BLOCKED (needed a workaround).

### The Go require resolves: CLEAN, and now rides v0.2.0

`go/go.mod` requires `github.com/Techrocket9/fklua/guest/go v0.1.0` with no replace. `go mod tidy` printed `go: downloading github.com/Techrocket9/fklua/guest/go v0.1.0`, exited 0, and wrote a two-line go.sum. At that moment the scaffold's guest.go imported the module behind `//go:build tinygo.wasm` and tidy kept the require (tidy considers all build tags). The real channel works first try. Caveat recorded by the Go review: once guest.go became a placeholder with no import, `go mod tidy` would DELETE the require and both go.sum lines; the require becomes load-bearing again when the emit layer imports fkdata. Do not run tidy in the placeholder window. The window closed with the emit layer: `go mod tidy -diff` exits 0 with no diff. Moved to `guest/go v0.2.0` for the raise import: `go mod tidy` downloaded it and exited 0, and the example module's own go.mod moved with it. Both go.sum pairs rewritten.

### The cargo git dependency finds fkdata: CLEAN, and now rides the same head as the harness

`rust/Cargo.toml` declares `fkdata = { git = "https://github.com/Techrocket9/fklua" }`, wasm-gated. `cargo fetch` updated the git repository and locked fkdata 0.1.0 plus its fk dependency at commit efcc71e8, exit 0. Cargo found the named package with no root Cargo.toml in that repo (the workspace root is guest/rust/) by scanning; fkdata's own `fk = { path = "../fk" }` resolved inside the fetched checkout. Cargo.lock is committed, matching FkLua's guest/rust convention. ASYMMETRY CLOSED: the lock pinned efcc71e8 while the harness built fklua from whatever `FKLUA_CHECKOUT` happened to be, so the library and the tool it is packaged by could skew silently. `cargo update -p fkdata -p fk` moved both to 83c62cd4, which is the checkout head the harness builds from; the library dependency and the harness now ride the same commit, and a future skew shows up as a lock diff rather than as a mystery. The skew showed up exactly that way one round later: FkLua's pushed head moved to ac0ebcc (two commits, a drafts note and fk_module.map.json in packaging output) while the lock still said 83c62cd4, and the lock was moved again to match. The FkLua-side doc commit for this repository's raise-prefix ask sits one commit ahead of the pushed head until the operator pushes; it changes comments and docs only, so the harness built from the checkout and the library pinned to the pushed head behave identically, and the lock moves once more after that push.

### `fklua init --library` for two languages: CLEAN, with the refusal doing the teaching

Running `fklua init --library fkrecipes --lang go,rust` refuses with:

    fklua init: --library scaffolds ONE language per directory; a two-language library is two sibling trees with a mirror test between them (the fkipc arrangement), so run init --library twice, in sibling directories

Running it twice in `go/` and `rust/` scaffolded exactly the documented four Go files and two Rust files, refused nothing else, and printed the composition-contract closing message both times.

### The scaffold is control-stage flavored: MISLED (mildly), for a data-stage library. RESOLVED upstream

`init --library` has one flavor, and it is a control-stage library: the example surface is `OnTick`/`OnEvent(id, ptr) bool` routing, the guest half calls `fk.Log`, the Go guest imports `guest/go/fk`, the Rust Cargo.toml declares `fk` as the wasm-gated dependency, and the generated contract comments emphasize event routing and multiplayer join safety. A data-stage library needs `fkdata` in both manifests and has no events at all, so both dependency declarations and both guest halves were rewritten by hand. The parts that transfer anyway (route-never-own, the build-gate split, no_std under cfg, the fk/fkgc feature ban, the [patch] one-source note) transfer verbatim. A `--data` flavor, or one sentence in the closing message saying "a data-stage library swaps fk for fkdata", would have removed the detour. RESOLVED: `fklua init --library <name> --lang go --data` exists. Its contract text is better than what this library had in two places, and both were adopted into the package docs: "Never iterate a Go map to decide what to emit: the data stage crosses sorted, and a map walk is a per-client order" states the mechanism where ours stated only the consequence, and the DIAGNOSE WITH Raise clause is the rule this round adopted.

### The scaffold's go.mod placeholder: AWKWARD. RESOLVED upstream

The generated `module fkrecipes` line carries a comment saying to rename it to the publishing path, which is right, but says nothing about the sibling-directory arrangement it itself prescribed one refusal earlier: a module living in the `go/` subdirectory of a repo must carry the `/go` suffix in its module path (`github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go`) or consumers cannot fetch it. Someone following the refusal message into `go/` and the comment's "rename to the path you will publish under" can write a path `go get` will never resolve. One sentence in the comment would close the gap. RESOLVED upstream.

### fklua mod packages the examples first try: CLEAN, with two advisory notes. BOTH RESOLVED upstream

Packaging both example guests worked with no refusals and no surprises, and --report drove every harness assertion (.ok, .hooks.data exactly [fk_settings, fk_data], .hooks.control empty, the data-updates and final-fixes files correctly absent). Two notes for FkLua rather than defects here. First, the NaN advisory prints 22 NaN-sensitive operations before the success lines, naming THIS LIBRARY's internals ((*Lib).Emit 17 times, PlanData, toV, fromV) in every consumer's packaging output; it is harmless for fkrecipes (the planner refuses every non-finite value before it can reach an op, so the sign and payload loss is unobservable) but a consumer packaging any mod that uses this library sees a wall of text about code they did not write and cannot action. Second, .pruning reports members, events and defines as 0/0 with complete false, which is correct for a data-only mod with no fkapi but reads like a problem at a glance. Also cosmetic: the advisory renders the Go World interface's whole method set inline as a symbol name, three times; the cost of an interface where Rust's trait object has no such symbol.

BOTH RESOLVED upstream, re-measured by re-running the harness packaging on 83c62cd4. The NaN advisory now caps the wall: 25 operations reported, eight named entries, then `  ... and 17 more of the same class`, with the remedy paragraph kept intact ("Most programs never observe this. If yours does, recompile with --nan=exact, which preserves NaN bits at a substantial speed cost."). The pruning block now reads `"pruning": {"members": {"complete": true, "shipped": 0, "total": 0}, "events": {...}, "defines": {...}}` with `"api": {..., "table_attached": false, "table_bytes": 0}`, so the data-only case is no longer reported as incomplete: fixed as not-even-correct rather than merely reworded. No harness assertion or note read either field, verified rather than assumed: run-mirror reads `.ok`, `.hooks.data` and `.hooks.control` and nothing else from the report.

### The wasm-to-Lua size inversion, measured

Go example wasm 385327 bytes lowers to 1455118 bytes of Lua; Rust example wasm 79102 bytes lowers to 1465851 bytes. The Rust guest is 4.9x smaller as wasm and produces slightly MORE Lua, so wasm size is not a proxy for what a player downloads. Worth a docs line.

### The parity recipe out of tree: what transferred and what had to be rebuilt

docs/library-parity.md's shape transferred cleanly: two guests, one interpreter, byte-compared transcripts, a committed golden that fails rather than skips when missing. What the recipe could not give an out-of-tree repo: the strict stand-in itself (net-new, built engine-shaped with the measured 21-key defines list and validating data:extend, because a harness more forgiving than the engine is the recorded trap); the harness driving (shelling out to a freshly built fklua with --report as the machine surface, instead of FkLua's internal Go packages, which the internal/ rule walls off); and lua52f, which cannot be rebuilt out of tree and ships from the checkout via FKLUA_CHECKOUT. One pleasant surprise: lua52f takes a script path plus argv cleanly, so the stand-in is a committed file rather than the generated string the in-tree harness builds. One trap demonstrated during the harness's own red proofs: with the stand-in's validation removed, a smuggled malformed prototype still failed the run but with 'attempt to compare number with string' far from the cause; strictness is what keeps the diagnostic at the scene. And one bash lesson recorded: a failing left operand of && is exempt from set -e, so refusal assertions are written as explicit if blocks.

### The mirror review round

Both reviewers took the harness before it landed, and the execution-focused pass proved the round's defect with a chain worth keeping: the two diff-report pipelines each sat as the last command of an if body, so under pipefail a differing mirror killed the script before the FAIL accumulator, the banner, or the golden comparison ran; and because the --update copy was unconditional, the only thing preventing the capture of a divergent golden was that accident (the reviewer applied the natural one-line banner fix and watched --update commit a Go-only transcript while reporting FAILED). Fixed by making the report pipelines deliberately non-fatal and gating the capture on a clean accumulator. Also fixed from the round: tmp is wiped per run (a poisoned tmp/bin/fklua non-binary was sticky across runs behind a misleading message), the one remaining condemned bracket-and shape, golden-coverage gaps in the example (branches the golden could not protect: an enabled-from-start recipe, an omitted energy_required, the max_level copy end to end, EnabledBy's ON branch, single-bound NumericSpec, the description present-and-absent split), and the stand-in now asserts the two measured legality numbers on extend so a floor or unit-count regression fails in the harness rather than shipping. Two closing-round notes worth keeping: the implementer's own earlier red-proof output had shown the missing FAILED banner and was read as a pass because the exit code was 1 for the wrong reason (the exit status was checked, the output was not; both are part of a proof); and the brief's suggested fixture row physical-projectile-damage-1 was corrected to level 7, because in base 2.0 the level-1 damage techs carry a plain count and it is the final level that is formula-priced and infinite, so the fixture now asserts a shape the engine actually has. What held under attack: determinism across cold, warm and wiped runs; every refusal path loud with its remedy on a doctored PATH; anti-vacuity greps surviving mutations that keep both transcripts equal; refuse-before-compare ordering making stale-transcript comparisons unreachable; shellcheck clean; the serialiser proven canonical and locale-independent (probed under four locales; lua52f does not expose os.setlocale at all); and pin-transparency re-verified at the packaged-binary level.

### A data guest cannot raise its own message: fkdata surface gap, graded AWKWARD. RESOLVED upstream and adopted

The library's refusals (a cycle with its full path, an overwrite, a research_trigger CostOf) must stop the load loudly, and the design says failures raise at the stage naming the path, per fkdata's rule. But that rule is about the HOST's errors: read from runtime/lua/fk_data.lua and fk_rt.lua, a guest has no raise primitive at all. A panic compiles to a wasm trap, the trap surfaces as the Lua error "fklua trap: unreachable" (a code, not a message), and TinyGo on wasm-unknown has no print channel (at that time the built module imported exactly the seven fkdata functions plus env.fk_log, nothing else; it now imports eight, fkdata.raise having joined them). The loudest available shape is therefore fkdata.Log the full sentence, then trap: the player sees a load failure naming the mod, and the author finds the actual reason one line above the trap in factorio-current.log. It works, but the engine's error box shows the generic trap string rather than the library's carefully-built diagnostic. A guest-facing raise import in the fkdata ABI (message in, Lua error out, sorted with the stage name the way the host's own errors are) would let a validation library surface its message where the player is looking. Recorded as the round's headline ecosystem finding.

RESOLVED and adopted. `fkdata.Raise(msg)` / `fkdata::raise(msg) -> !` landed upstream and every diagnostic exit in this library now routes through it: `Emit`'s error path is a single `Raise` call, and the Log-then-panic shape and the comment narrative explaining it are deleted. The host prefixes the stage in `fk_data.lua`'s `fail()`, so the ADOPTION FORCED A MESSAGE RESHAPE across the library: refusals changed from `fkrecipes: at the <stage> stage, <what>` to `fkrecipes: <what>`, because a stage in both strings reaches the player twice in one sentence. 90 message expectations per language moved with them, symmetric to the count. What the player now reads, captured end to end through a packaged mod under the stand-in and byte-identical in both languages:

    fklua: at the data stage, fkrecipes: two technologies share the name hardened-tips; the second would overwrite the first

against what the same refusal produced before, where the error box carried a code and the sentence survived only as a log line:

    fklua trap: unreachable

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE World SEAM, decided rather than deferred: the stage was threaded into the planner for message-building alone, so with the prefix gone `StageName`/`stage_name` fed nothing. It is REMOVED from the trait in both languages. Keeping it "for the emit dispatch" would have been false comfort: the dispatch reads `fkdata.Stage()` directly and never asked the World, so the method was pure cost on every consumer who implements a World for their own host tests. The stage now has exactly one source, which is the host that prints it. `validate`, `validateSettings`, `checkResolvedCraftTimes` and `checkCycles` lost their stage parameters with it.

### The crafting-time floor, measured

Probed on Factorio 2.0.77 (build 84539, mac-arm64, steam; the binary re-asked its version before the runs), with a plain Lua probe mod under FACTORIO_USERDIR=/tmp/fkrecipes-probe and --dump-data:

- energy_required = 0 refuses the load, exit 1: `Error while loading recipe prototype "fkr-probe-zero" (recipe): energy_required can't be <= 0.001.`
- energy_required = -1 refuses identically (same message, exit 1).
- energy_required = 0.0011 and 0.002 both load (exit 0) and survive to data-raw-dump.json unchanged.
- An omitted energy_required stays absent in the dump (the engine applies its 0.5 default at runtime).

So the engine's floor is exclusive at 0.001: a value must be strictly greater. The library encodes this as a named floor constant (refuse plan values at or below it, quoting the engine's reason) and gives a generated crafting-time setting minimum_value = 0.002 when the consumer does not supply a minimum, so the settings GUI cannot produce a value that kills the next load. A consumer-supplied minimum at or below the floor is refused at plan time, and a bound value that arrives at or below the floor anyway (a colliding mod can silently overwrite a same-type setting name) refuses naming the setting rather than letting the engine blame the consumer.

### defines.prototypes.item membership, measured

Probed on Factorio 2.0.77 (build 84539, mac-arm64, steam; version re-asked), plain Lua probe mod logging the sorted keys of defines.prototypes.item at the data stage: exactly 21 keys, and "item" itself IS among them (ammo, armor, blueprint, blueprint-book, capsule, copy-paste-tool, deconstruction-item, gun, item, item-with-entity-data, item-with-inventory, item-with-label, item-with-tags, module, rail-planner, repair-tool, selection-tool, space-platform-starter-pack, spidertron-remote, tool, upgrade-item). Measured because the emit layer's ItemExists walk was found resting on this unmeasured fact; the fix probes the plain item type first so the walk no longer depends on it.

### The emit review round

Both reviewers took the emit layer before it landed. The Rust reviewer compiled and linted everything on the wasm target and measured conversion recursion depth against the module's real stack layout (4141 levels survivable; the host caps nesting at 64 anyway). The Go reviewer went further and RAN the module: a Node stand-in for fkdata's seven imports faithful to the tier-2 wire, so its findings are executed, not inferred. Converging headline defect, shared by both halves with no divergence: fromV silently dropped number-keyed map pairs behind a comment claiming the host shim refuses them; fk_data.lua's key_rank accepts numeric keys, any holed or mixed Lua table crosses as a numeric-keyed map, and a copied unit with a holed ingredients list would emit a technology researchable for free. Go-side extras: the ItemExists assumption above; the Emit doc comment inviting a two-data-hook call that hard-fails as an overwrite; a measured memory profile (linear in plan size as designed, but about 13.6 KiB leaked per emitted prototype, 11606 host gets at plan size 400, crossing FkLua's documented 782 ms memory.grow wall at 16 MiB). What held at the binary level, verified from the built module's sections: exactly the fkdata imports of the day (seven then, eight now that fkdata.raise is adopted) plus env.fk_log, clone dead-code eliminated, no library-owned export, pin-transparency and route-never-own both proven on the artifact rather than the source. The refusal path was verified end to end: at that time the full message reached factorio-current.log through the unbuffered fk_log import and was followed by a trap; it now leaves through fkdata.Raise instead. A refusal never leaves a partial write because validation precedes the first Extend, which is unchanged.

The fix round closed everything: unfaithful copies now refuse by name (a map with any non-string key converts to Nil as a whole subtree, and the pure half walks every CostOf-copied unit, arrays included, refusing on the Nil marker, which fkdata's write side can never produce legitimately); ItemExists probes the plain item type first with the measured defines fact in the comment, the DerivedTypes read hoisted to World construction and a linear-scan memo removing both multipliers the review measured; the Emit doc names the one-data-family-hook contract; Rust's from_v arms are exhaustive so a new fkdata variant breaks the build; the empty-pack-name and nil-max_level holes are closed in both halves (the max_level hole existed in Rust too, not only Go as first reported); a pure invariant test pins that no Set op ever carries nil (which fkdata.Set would turn into a key deletion). Flash cost of the round: 349671 to 356290 bytes (+1.9 percent). One process near-miss recorded: a tail-edit almost deleted four unrelated tests; the compiler's unused-import errors caught it and the tests were recovered from git, a reminder that surgical edits beat file-tail rewrites.

Final confirmations, both executed: the holed-unit copy refuses by name with zero extends; plain items resolve with defines absent entirely; the memo halved host gets at plan size 400 (11606 to 5218) while memory stayed 16 MiB, so the memory.grow hazard note stands as written; the RefCell memo survives even a hypothetical reentrant probe; a variant added to fkdata's V breaks the Rust build (proven with a vendored fkdata plus [patch]). Two small carry-overs assigned to the next round rather than papered over: holdsDroppedSubtree does not yet walk a map-shaped max_level (cosmetic; the field is a number or "infinite" in practice), and the tech_unit-returns-Nil arm refuses correctly but has no fixture route in the crate's own suite (proven with a hand-rolled World).

### The library's flash cost, measured

A bare fkdata-only TinyGo guest is 42280 bytes of flash; the same guest planning and emitting through fkrecipes is 349671 bytes, so the library costs a consumer about 300 KB (tinygo -target=wasm-unknown -scheduler=none -gc=leaking -opt=2, 2026-08-31). Worth stating in the docs.

### `; echo exit=$?` is a session convention, not an FkLua script convention

The instruction to never read a gate's exit code through a pipe is recorded here as adopted for operator commands. FkLua's scripts themselves guard differently (`set -euo pipefail`, redirect-to-log with `|| { cat log; return 1; }`, a FAIL accumulator); this repo's scripts follow the FkLua shape, and the `; echo exit=$?` capture is used when a human or agent runs a gate by hand.

## The in-game gate

scripts/run-ingame.sh runs the packaged examples under the real engine (re-asked its version first: 2.0.77 build 84539), under FACTORIO_USERDIR=/tmp/fkrecipes so a running game's lock cannot kill the gate, and pins one golden line per engine: both normalised dump hashes (the settings dump hashed too, because setting prototypes never reach the data dump and a one-hash golden is green for a silent fk_settings; FkLua's recorded lesson) plus the mod set. Go and Rust produce identical hashes in-game; the whole gate is 17 to 18 seconds including builds; a mod-set mismatch reports SKIPPED loudly and exits 0 per the FkLua convention (a CI flag to make SKIPPED nonzero is recorded as open).

The phase's findings. First, the expected stand-in-versus-world question resolved in the stand-in's favour: base 2.0.77 really carries physical-projectile-damage-7 with count_formula 2^(L-7)*1000 and max_level infinite, exactly as the mirror models it; the gate now asserts CostOf's copy against the source prototype in the same dump rather than a transcribed number, so it checks faithfulness, not expectation. The stand-in's two deliberate flavour differences (pack list and prerequisites) are recorded as such. Second, an assertion the implementer got wrong and the library got right: in-game there is no mod-settings.dat, so EnabledBy reads its declared default and the technology comes out enabled with no hidden key; the switched-off branch belongs to the mirror, whose stand-in flips the setting. The two gates therefore cover opposite sides of the hidden-not-absent decision, and neither could cover both alone; the script says so in a comment. The ladder-drop log line fires in real base too (no tungsten-carbide or titanium-plate there either), so fkdata.Log is exercised through the engine's own log.

The script review sustained five findings, fixed before the commit: a single-line local declaration evaluating a sibling assignment from the enclosing scope (working only because the caller's loop variable shared the name; the repo's first shellcheck warning); the hash pipeline wrapped in an echo command substitution, so a malformed dump would have produced the empty-input constant hash for both languages, satisfied the mirror and determinism checks, and could have reached a golden through --update (reworked to a normalised temp file with no pipes, the house lesson applied to its own gate); the userdir hardcoded while the docs described an env var (now an overridable default); run-mirror's tmp wipe taking run-ingame's subtree with it (both scripts now own disjoint subtrees); and two preconditions failing in bash's voice rather than the script's. What held: three-run determinism from a fully cold userdir, every cheap refusal loud with its remedy, the fully guarded --update flow, SKIPPED unable to mask a same-run FAIL, all ten jq assertions non-vacuous with real values on both sides, and a golden whose recorded mod set is byte-identical to what the engine's log actually said.

## Design deviations

Every deviation lands here with the evidence that forced it.

- docs-style.md is carried as a copy of FkLua's with one section adapted: the licence-statements paragraph named FkLua's third-party inputs (testdata/spec, third_party/lua-5.2.1), which do not exist here, so it now states FkRecipes' own MIT position. Everything else is verbatim.
- The em-dash and en-dash ban is extended to working notes and every error message (the parents exempt working notes). Recorded in CLAUDE.md's critical rules.
- **max_level is not a unit field.** The design says CostOf copies "the whole unit including count_formula/max_level verbatim", but the engine keeps max_level on the technology prototype, beside unit, not inside it. A verbatim unit copy therefore cannot carry it. Resolution: World grew TechMaxLevel (a Value, since the engine's field is a number or the string "infinite"), and CostOf copies it onto the generated technology separately; count_formula rides inside the unit copy as the design expected. The unit copy itself is proven a copy by an unrecognised key surviving byte for byte.
- **The sketch's functional options became spec structs and typed 1-based handles**, so the two languages mirror field for field (Go zero values and Rust Default both mean absent) and a reference to an undeclared or foreign thing is refused rather than representable. The dropdown constructor is named DropdownSettingNeedingLocale; the name is the design's required mark that dropdown values have no inline localisation.
- **Overwrite is refused, not performed.** A planned item, recipe or technology whose prefixed name already exists in data.raw is a refusal, not a silent data:extend overwrite. This also closes a cycle-validator blind spot found in review: a duplicate node in the overlay would bind incoming edges to the stale copy and accept a real cycle. Consequence, to document for consumers: Emit runs from exactly one data-stage hook.
- **TechSpec.AfterTech is a surface addition.** The design's InsertBetween/After anchor only external names, so a plan could not chain its own technologies (the anchor probe against data.raw logged a drop and detached the chain). AfterTech anchors on a plan-declared technology by handle. An AfterTech edge cannot close a cycle: the handle only exists after the technology it names, so the edges run backwards through declaration order, and Before with AfterTech is refused so nothing existing is rewritten to require the new node. The argument is recorded at the branch in both halves.
- **A pre-existing cycle in the world's own tree is refused with the full path** even when the plan adds no edge to it. The design only demands the overlay walk; refusing the inherited cycle is strictly more information than the engine's eight-word abort, which the load was heading for anyway.

## The adversarial review rounds

Maintainer rule, applied retroactively: all Rust must pass adversarial review before commit; after the Rust rounds kept finding real defects, the maintainer extended the pass to the Go half, informed by the Rust catalogue. The Go round found three blockers the Rust pass structurally could not see: the per-Lib id counter was a plain package var (proven racy: 4662 duplicate ids under 20000 concurrent New calls, reopening silent cross-plan handle resolution for consumers running parallel tests; fixed with sync/atomic and a -race gate), all seven handle types were mutually convertible because Go treats identically-shaped structs as identical types (fkrecipes.BoolSettingRef(anIntSettingRef) compiled in a consumer package and silently hid a technology; fixed with distinct zero-size marker fields), and the two languages' shortest-round-trip float formatters break representation ties differently, so host transcripts diverged on real float paths including the verbatim CostOf unit copy (6118 divergences per 1.15M matched samples, with a formatter-parity test that could not fail; fixed by replacing both test formatters with one deterministic 17-significant-digit rule). It also found: Lib copyable by value with two copies appending into one backing array (fixed with a noCopy vet guard), specs aliasing caller slices so one unchanged Lib could give two different plans (fixed by copying on declaration), missing sign guards on every amount but the unit count plus a negative CraftTime silently swallowed (fixed with refusals), silent rounding of int64 amounts above 2^53 (fixed with a refusal at the exact boundary), a unit-shape guard on CostOf, Go returning live slices where Rust clones, and a nil-World panic (Go-only guard; a Rust &dyn World cannot be null). The TinyGo build tag, the message parity (every refusal string byte-identical across the halves), the full re-run of the Rust attack catalogue against Go, and the pure planner's TinyGo compile (12340 flash bytes, no reflect, no fmt, no sort) all held. After the guard round the library measures 13914 flash bytes (+12.8 percent, the guards and their message strings; sync/atomic cost 23 bytes, the handle markers cost zero).

One symmetry to leave alone: Go carries a nil-World guard that Rust cannot reach (&dyn World is never null), while Rust carries a zero-id Lib check that safe Rust cannot reach (Default is gone; kept for mirror symmetry with Go, whose zero value cannot be suppressed). These are opposite policies about unreachable guards, each right for its language; do not tidy either away.

Honest caveats from the closing round, recorded rather than smoothed: the noCopy guard on Lib converts a silent corruption into a vet finding, and copylocks is not in go test's default vet subset, so a consumer who never runs go vet can still copy a Lib and corrupt both copies' plans; that is the standard Go idiom's ceiling, stated so "fixed" is not read as "unrepresentable". A typed-nil World still panics (no interface nil check can see it; Go-inherent, no Rust equivalent). The defensive clones (Go cloning where Rust clones) carry no red proof, because the aliased lists are read-only downstream and removing the clones changes no output; that is mirror parity, not a fixed bug. The refusal-ordering fix's originally-briefed test shape was unreachable (two invalid no-name recipes refuse on the first result check before any duplicate scan) and the vacuous first test was replaced with the reachable shape. Two thresholds sit deliberately one apart: validation accepts amounts up to and including 2^53 while the transcript formatter's plain-digit path stops strictly below it, so the boundary value renders in scientific form, identically in both halves. Left open for the next phase: a declaration with a literally empty name is not refused yet. The first review (whole rust/ tree, differential-fuzzed against the Go mirror with matched LCG generators, 8000 cases, zero divergence) confirmed the core and found: two blockers (EnabledBy indexed without validation, a reachable panic from a foreign handle; the example placeholder not compiling for wasm because nothing linked the panic handler), three defects (the cycle blind spot above; in-range handles from another Lib silently resolving to the wrong declaration; no intra-plan chaining), and hardening items (per-Lib handle tags, non-finite float refusals including UnitSpec.Seconds, empty mod name refusals, a 100-node cap on the cycle path message, Go int widths moved to int64 because TinyGo's wasm int is 32 bits, fixture units re-sorted to the order fkdata actually hands back, resolver/profile/autoexamples added to rust/Cargo.toml, the host-gate comment corrected: cargo resolves and clones the git graph even for a host test run, so that gate needs the network once). One review item accepted as-is: the Op stream's payloads are public and therefore forgeable; the op stream is the emit layer's internal seam and a consumer forging ops is out of contract. One requested red proof was correctly refused by the implementer: a cycle through AfterTech edges is unreachable by construction (argument above).

One proof vacuity was found and fixed during the round: the guard keeping max_level off hand-rolled units could not go red because the fixture answered "no cap" for the empty name; the fixture now answers for any name, making the guard observable. Lesson recorded: a red proof that stays green indicts the test first.

The verification round then found the fix code's own gaps, and they were closed too: autoexamples was in the wrong TOML table (a [package] key under [lib], silently ignored and warning on every build including consumers'); Lib::default() and Go's zero-value Lib bypassed the new id tag (Rust dropped the Default derive, and both languages refuse a zero-id Lib at both entry points; clippy's new_without_default demand was refused with the reason inline, since a Default is exactly the defect); and plan_settings taking the mod name as a parameter could drift from what plan_data derives, so the parameter is gone and both entry points take the World. The AfterTech no-cycle comment was reworded to make the overlay the load-bearing defence. Phase totals: 31 mirrored test functions per language, 109 injected defects across all rounds, zero that failed to go red (stale patch strings after churn were restated and re-run). The reviewer's informational items stand recorded: World.recipe_exists is a new required method with no default (correct pre-1.0, the only implementor is the unwritten emit layer), and the cycle walk's linear node scan measures 658 microseconds at base-game scale, 42 ms at 3000 technologies.

## The crafting-time review round

Both reviewers took the binding before it landed. What held is the load-bearing part: the floor literals are bit-identical across the languages (0x3f50624dd2f1a9fc and 0x3f60624dd2f1a9fc), the boundary is exclusive to the exact ulp in both directions on all three guarded paths, the marking scan neither cross-marks nor mis-marks under 20 rebuilds and 40-recipe fan-in, and the extended differential corpora (a 136-line boundary corpus by bit pattern plus the 4000-case generators grown to the new surface) diff empty. Findings, all fixed in the closing round: a bound crafting time answered as +Inf passed the floor check and shipped a recipe that never completes, the one float path that never crossed the finiteness guard (a colliding mod's math.huge default is the reachable route); NaN refused with the floor message rather than a finiteness one; the foreign-handle skip in the marking scan had no in-suite red-provable witness (every test stayed green under mutation, this repo's own named failure shape); auto-minimum bounds refusals blamed a minimum the consumer never declared; and one accountability catch: the unit-is-Nil fixture route reported as landed in the previous round did not actually exist (the behaviour was right, the coverage claim was not), caught because the reviewer re-verified the claim instead of trusting the report. Lesson recorded beside the red-proof rule: a coverage claim is verified by mutating the covered code, not by reading the report that says it is covered. The closing round fixed all four (finiteness checked before the floor with its own message; an in-suite witness whose mutation flips exactly as predicted; the real present-with-nil fixture route landed beside the absent row; generated-minimum refusals name the generator), and kept one third arm deliberately unchanged: when a declared maximum sits below a declared default, both compared ends are the consumer's own, so the existing message is the correct attribution.

## The locale checker

BetterBeltBalancer's tripwire standardised: pure, host-testable, both directions, in both languages, pinned to one committed golden over one committed fixture cfg (the design's cheapest cross-language pin, working with no toolchain installed). Parsing semantics are BBB's, which are the engine's, with the subtle one carried deliberately: the key is NOT trimmed around the equals sign, because Factorio takes everything before the first equals as the key, so a checker that tidied whitespace would pass a file the game reads differently. Two checks beyond the brief because BBB's parser reports them (an unparseable line, a key defined twice in one section), emitted first because a file the parser could not read makes every later finding suspect. One stated limitation rather than a silent gap: the [mod-setting-name] orphan rule can only police by mod prefix, so a setting the consumer wrote by hand in the same file reports as an orphan; the doc comment says so, and an opt-out is left for a consumer to ask for. The golden's four findings were predicted by hand before the implementation produced them, byte-identical from both halves.

The review round split usefully: the reference-diffing pass caught the one semantic defect (duplicate-key presence answered from any non-blank entry while the finding text promised last-wins, a false negative on exactly the shape a blank sneaks in through; BBB's overwrite semantics catch it), while the hostile-input pass proved what held (the cross-language Unicode whitespace tables identical across 13 probed codepoints, no UTF-8 slicing hazard, case-sensitive keys correctly not colliding) and measured the O(n squared) duplicate scan to 2.9 seconds at 50000 distinct keys. Fixed together: last-entry-wins presence; a missing-entry finding that names the section the key actually sits under (the precise form of the mis-cased-section hint; a blanket unknown-section finding would false-positive on every legitimate [item-name] section); a leading BOM reported once and stripped for the rest of the parse, with the comment stating the engine's BOM behaviour is unmeasured so the checker must not silently bless one; empty section headers and empty keys reported per the parser's own stated rule; a sorted-index duplicate scan and a 100-finding cap (the cycle-path precedent); quoting for keys that differ from their trimmed form. One deliberate divergence from the reference confirmed and documented: descriptions stay optional, because the engine's failure mode for a missing description is a lost tooltip, not an Unknown key render.

## Repository decisions

- Trunk is `master` (renamed from the freshly-initialized `main` before the first commit; matches the house convention and FkLua's /blob/master/ link scheme).
- The Go example guest will be its own module and the Rust example guest is a workspace member with wasm-gated dependencies, so `go test ./...` and `cargo test` on the host never try to compile fkdata (which cannot build off-target in either language, by design).

## Gate results

Recorded per commit as the gates land. Bootstrap baseline, 2026-08-31:

- `cd go && go mod tidy` exit 0 (network fetch of the v0.1.0 tag)
- `cd go && go test ./...` exit 0 (scaffold test)
- `cd rust && cargo fetch` exit 0 (git resolution)
- `cd rust && cargo test` exit 0 (scaffold test, 1 passed)

Planner and validators, 2026-08-31, after the adversarial round:

- `cd go && gofmt -l .` empty, `go vet ./...` exit 0, `go test ./...` exit 0, `go test -race ./...` exit 0 (35 test functions; the four beyond Rust's 31 cover Go-native hazards that are compile-time impossibilities in Rust: concurrent ids, two aliasing shapes, nil World)
- `cd rust && cargo fmt --check` exit 0, `cargo test` exit 0 (31 passed), `cargo clippy --lib` exit 0
- `cd rust && cargo build --target wasm32-unknown-unknown --workspace` exit 0 (the library and the example placeholder both compile for the shipping target; this gate exists because cargo test alone builds the std host shape and reads green over a wasm break)
- Red proofs across the phase: over 130 proofs across five rounds (initial planner, max_level, Rust review fixes, zero-id and seam fixes, Go review fixes), mirrored across the languages where both halves share the defect, spanning injected defects, compile-failure proofs (handle forgeries, Lib copies under vet) and a -race witness; zero failed to go red, and stale patch strings after churn were restated and re-run rather than trusted.

## The migration path (maintainer scope addition, 2026-08-31)

Mods with hand-rolled user settings must be able to switch to FkRecipes without losing player preferences, with BetterBeltBalancer as the pilot. The engine fact that shapes everything: Factorio persists startup values in mod-settings.dat by setting NAME, with no rename mechanism, so preferences survive exactly when the emitted names do not change. BBB's recon made the crux concrete: its packaged name is better-belt-balancer but all three settings carry the historical bbb- prefix, so none migrates by name-matching and every one needs an explicit legacy-name escape hatch; renames are proven harmful there (startup values silently reset, and the runtime-global bool is written per save by the mod's own grandfather pass). The recon also showed BBB's two cost settings drive exactly what the design charter promised settings could drive (an option-selected ingredient plan per dropdown value; a setting-chosen cost source whose prerequisite follows the copied unit), so the migration path adds those two charter verbs beside the Legacy constructors. Out of scope and documented as such: BBB's runtime-global engine-gated bool is a control-stage concern that stays hand-rolled beside lib.Emit in the mod's own fk_settings hook (the hook belongs to the mod; composition, not migration). The migration's acceptance criterion is BBB's own mod_settings_sha256 golden: a migrated settings stage must hash identically, which pins names, types, defaults, orders and allowed_values byte for byte. The end-to-end legacy exercise belongs to the BBB pilot in its own repository with its own goldens; this repository covers the Legacy constructors with unit and locale tests, and the two charter verbs ride the mirror and engine gates through the example.

The implementation round's own findings: the new tests caught two real bugs immediately (the enablement and crafting-time resolution paths built setting names as prefix plus name directly instead of through the single emittedName seam, so a legacy setting was looked up under a name it never had; both languages, both fixed); two pre-existing mirror anti-vacuity assertions were themselves vacuous (the max_level and count_formula greps were satisfied by the stand-in's own source row whether or not anything was copied) and are now anchored to the generated prototype, with the cap-and-formula coverage sitting on the engine gate where the copy is compared against the source in the same dump; and one check was kept without a red proof and said so (the ladder's exists condition is not independently observable, because a technology that does not exist also carries no unit, and a vacuous green test claiming coverage would be worse than the honest gap). The two ladders split across the gates deliberately: the mirror's stand-in flips the new dropdown and walks the military row, the engine gate runs on defaults and walks past a rung no vanilla install has to copy physical-projectile-damage-7 verbatim; neither gate could cover both. Both goldens were regenerated through their own --update flows, stated rather than slipped in.

## The FkLua alignment round

FkLua acted on all five dogfooding asks, and this round incorporates them. The dependency moved through the real channels rather than a checkout: `guest/go v0.2.0` in the library module and in the example module, and `cargo update -p fkdata -p fk` off the locked efcc71e8 onto 83c62cd4. That commit is the head the harness's `FKLUA_CHECKOUT` builds `fklua` from, so the library dependency and the tool that packages it now ride one commit; the asymmetry that let them skew silently is closed, and a future skew is a lock diff.

The adoption's real cost was not the call site. `Raise` routes a guest's own message through the host's stage-prefixed `fail()`, so every refusal template in the library had to STOP CARRYING ITS OWN STAGE, the validator internals that threaded the stage name had to give it up, and `World.StageName` fell out as dead. That is recorded in full under the graded raise entry above, including the removal decision and what the player reads before and after.

Verification worth keeping. The import set was re-decoded from both freshly packaged modules rather than inferred: EIGHT imports each, identical sets, `fkdata.raise` joining `env.fk_log`, `fkdata.env`, `extend`, `get`, `keys`, `set` and `stage`. The end-to-end raise was proven per language through a packaged mod under the strict stand-in, and byte-identical across them. The double-prefix hazard the reshape exists to avoid was demonstrated rather than argued: putting the stage back into one message template produced `fklua: at the data stage, fkrecipes: at the data stage, two technologies share the name hardened-tips` end to end, and restoring it produced the single prefix again.

A review finding on this round's own test, worth recording because the mistake is a common one: the six-case behavioural test was named as a sweep and was a sample. The reviewer put the stage back into the technology-overwrite template, a template none of the six plans reaches, and got the double-prefixed line end to end while both halves stayed green. Roughly eighty message chunks per language cannot be covered by six plans, so the sweep is now a SOURCE-TEXT PROPERTY in each language, on FkLua's own precedent: the Go half walks the package's non-test files with go/ast and checks every STRING BasicLit (363 literals across 10 files), the Rust half reads src/*.rs and runs a small comment-and-literal state machine (329 literals across 10 modules). Scoping to string literals is load-bearing rather than tidy: " stage, " occurs in ordinary wrapped prose throughout both halves' comments, so a raw substring scan over the source would be all false positives. The six behavioural cases are kept and renamed to what they prove, that a refusal COMPOSES into something the host can prefix; neither test replaces the other, since the property cannot tell whether a message composes and the six cannot tell whether every template obeys. Both properties were red-proved with the reviewer's exact mutation and name the offending file, line and literal.

Neither golden moved. The mirror transcript and the two engine dump hashes are unchanged, which is the expected result and worth stating: the happy path carries no refusal, so an exit-path change has nothing to move there. The raising paths are proven in the host harness instead, where a refusal can actually be provoked.

## The adoption review round (the FkLua v0.2.0 incorporation)

Both reviewers took the Raise adoption before its commit. What held, proven rather than asserted: the raise path end to end through the real packaged mods and the real fk_data.lua, one host stage prefix, the library sentence verbatim, UTF-8 crossing byte-exact including astral codepoints, zero prototypes emitted before a refusal; the 100-node cycle cap still bounding the wire at about 1.2 KB regardless of ring size; the error arm proven at the type level in Rust (patching upstream raise to return unit breaks the build, so a refused plan cannot execute); the stale-shim trap failing loud (a pre-v0.2.0 shim link-errors at instantiation naming the missing import, and both scripts rebuild fklua from the checkout every run, so the packaged shim always matches the module); 181 distinct refusals produced across the corpora with zero carrying a stage and zero missing the fkrecipes attribution; and both goldens byte-identical to their committed copies, since the happy path carries no refusal. One finding sustained, on the guard itself: the destaged-refusal test was a six-case behavioural sample wearing a sweep's name, and an injected stage in an unswept template walked past it in both halves; closed with the house-precedent text property over every message literal in the source, with the behavioural cases kept under an honest name. One ask recorded for FkLua: raise's doc comment should say the host prefixes the stage, because nothing in the signature reveals it and a library with stage-prefixed messages says it twice with nothing failing. CLOSED upstream the same day, FkLua master 32eb628 (not yet pushed): both doc comments, the shim header, docs/data-stage.md and both --data scaffold templates now quote the prefix text and the corollary that a refusal built in a library carries no stage, the scaffold pins are extended and red-proven, the in-tree raise test asserts the exact composed line instead of two substrings, and a drift guard reads the prefix out of fail() by regexp and requires every doc surface to quote it. FkLua's correction to the ask, recorded as given: the surfaces already said the stage was prefixed; what was missing was the prefix's text, that the message lands verbatim behind it, and the corollary.

## The finish-out review round

The closing sweep (CheckLocaleWith, the engine gate's --strict, the min-only golden coverage, the two-Lib stage split) went through both reviewers before its commit. What held: CheckLocaleWith through BetterBeltBalancer's real shape (its two legacy dropdowns declared, the runtime-global bool hand-rolled, its shipped cfg validating clean, and a stale renamed legacy name now caught, closing the limit recorded two rounds earlier); the list creating no obligations, duplicates and empty strings harmless, the value direction unchanged, collisions reported first, message parity byte for byte over a 68-line corpus; the strict flag matrix (default SKIPPED exit 0, --strict and FKRECIPES_STRICT=1 exit 1, real set exit 0, unknown and near-miss flags rejected loudly); the min-only setting with its declared minimum standing instead of the generated floor-safe one (proven by mutating both examples so the transcripts stay equal and only the grep can fire); the two-Lib fixture anchored to plan A's asserted transcript so a name drift fails loudly; and every standing corpus identical across the halves on the realigned pin. Two findings, both closed before the commit: an empty hand-rolled list means complete-list policing with nothing hand-rolled (the strict reading, and the right one, but undocumented; a consumer reaching for nil expecting the plain call would get the opposite, and another mod's entries in a shared file are reported too), now stated in both doc comments and the usage page; and --strict guarded the comparison but not the capture, so a job-wide FKRECIPES_STRICT=1 with --update could record a golden from the wrong DLC world at exit 0, now refused under strict while plain --update keeps re-recording after an environment change.

## Handoff to the operator

No remote exists and nothing is pushed. When the repository goes to GitHub, the Go half's release tag must be `go/v0.1.0` (Go's convention for a module rooted in a subdirectory: the tag carries the directory prefix, or `go get github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go@v0.1.0` resolves nothing), and the Rust half needs no tag (git dependency plus [patch], the house convention). Until then a consumer (the BetterBeltBalancer pilot first) takes the Go module by replace onto a checkout and the Rust crate by path, marked dev-only, exactly as this repository's own examples do; the README already shows both forms. After the operator pushes FkLua's 32eb628, this repository's cargo lock moves once more to match.

## The closing review round

The last two closures went through both reviewers. The exists deletion held under the sharpest attack available: the only World that can disagree with the unit arm is one returning a unit for a technology whose name leaf is absent, which through the real emit layer needs a nameless table assigned directly into data.raw (data:extend refuses a prototype with no name; the mirror's own stand-in asserts it and the engine enforces it), so the change is confined to a state Factorio cannot reach, and the shim's resolve() is what makes an absent prototype answer absent on every path (proven at the shim and through the wire stand-in). The research_trigger arm's ordering ahead of the unit read was red-proven load-bearing, the one shape where the two arms can disagree. The stage dispatch was driven through both packaged modules with seven stage ids: the four known ones plan, every unknown id raises exactly once with zero extends and the byte-identical sentence under one host prefix, and the mapping was checked byte for byte against the StageID names in the cached v0.2.0 that actually ships. One finding held the round to its own standard: the unit arm's ok term was, like the deleted check, a guard no in-repo test could make load-bearing (every shipped World returns Nil beside false, so the shape test already covers absence); it is not dead, because a consumer's World could return a map beside ok=false, so instead of arguing it a fixture arm now answers exactly that contract violation and a mirrored test steps past it, red-proven by dropping the term. The standard is now uniform: every guard in the ladder has its own witness.

## The consumer round: BetterBeltBalancer's report, graded by the consumer

The pilot migrated its two startup dropdowns onto LegacyDropdownSettingNeedingLocale, emitted from its own fk_settings hook, and preserved every player's preference: BBB's mod_settings_sha256 golden did not move on either mod set (base and incumbent, 2.0.77 row), with data_raw unmoved too because the content stayed hand-rolled. The runtime-global bool stayed beside the Emit call as designed. Measured cost of adopting the library on BBB: zip 583756 to 665986 bytes (+14.1 percent), fk_data_module.lua 1763788 to 3216828 bytes (+82.4 percent), about +0.10 s parse per load across three stages, nothing on any tick moved. Six red proofs on the consumer side, an adversarial review with no correctness defect.

The content half (item, recipe, technology) could not migrate, measured on a scratch clone that the engine refused to load: `Error in assignID: item with name 'bbb-balancer-part' does not exist. Source: bbb-balancer-part (simple-entity-with-force).` The consumer's grades, verbatim in substance:

- BLOCKED: prototype names are always prefixed and there is no Legacy form for items, recipes or technologies, so docs/migration.md's incremental step 2 cannot be taken by its own worked example. The renamed item breaks the hand-rolled entity that names it, and a saved game references prototype names exactly as it references setting names.
- BLOCKED: no order slot on ItemSpec, RecipeSpec or TechSpec, and no place_result on ItemSpec; all four fields measured DROPPED from the dump.
- BLOCKED: Recipe needs an ItemRef from the same plan, so a recipe cannot migrate without its item.
- BLOCKED with a one-flag workaround: Emit sits behind //go:build tinygo.wasm, so the standard toolchain's vet reports Emit undefined on the consumer's data guest; BBB's make check now passes -tags tinygo.wasm on that one line.
- AWKWARD: Emit dispatches on the stage at run time, so a settings-only guest links PlanData as a 21047-line Lua function that never runs; an EmitSettings/EmitData split is the ask. Also: go mod tidy deletes an unimported require; the fklua require moves v0.0.0 to v0.2.0 by MVS under the replace; World has ten methods where PlanSettings needs one; no exported locale reader for a consumer's own extra assertions; the settings-only plan shape is undocumented.
- MISLED: the README's 320 KiB flash figure understates what ships (+1.45 MB of Lua); migration.md's "without changing anything a player can observe" holds for settings only; the build tag is named nowhere; CheckLocaleWith's claim that a wrong mod name fails loudly is silently untrue for a fully-legacy plan (measured: zero findings, because no prefixed name exists to miss).
- CLEAN: the Legacy setting constructors, CheckLocaleWith, PlanSettings on the host, IngredientsBy and CostBy (correct ingredients, unit and prerequisite when driven by hand), and the engine's silence on a clean plan.

The consumer also re-established its FkLua baseline on the checkout head 32eb628: gen-bindings and lock checks unmoved, api pin 2.1.17 unchanged, the only packaging change the additive fkdata.raise import plus two debug map files. Its full record is BetterBeltBalancer's agents/fkrecipes-migration.md.

### The answer round: every grade acted on

All four BLOCKED items and all six AWKWARD ones are closed, and the four MISLED documentation claims corrected. What landed:

Legacy PROTOTYPES, the same escape hatch the settings got and for a stronger reason: LegacyItem, LegacyRecipe and LegacyTechnology emit the name verbatim, the returned handles are the ordinary typed ones so a plan can be half migrated, and the duplicate scans and overwrite refusals moved to the EMITTED names, which is the namespace the engine keeps. The refusal messages for duplicates now name the emitted string rather than the declared one, matching what the settings scan already did.

Field slots: Order on all three specs, PlaceResult on the item with a real presence probe behind a new World method (EntityExists, walking DerivedTypes("entity") with the item probe's shape and memo, read LAZILY because a plan with no PlaceResult should not pay for the call), and Extra on all three, passed through verbatim after the library's own fields. Extra refuses a key the library emits itself, a key set twice, and an empty key; a collision is a silent last-writer otherwise, and the loser would be whichever append order the library happens to use. The own-field lists are written out rather than derived from what a given declaration emitted, because a refusal that depended on which conditional arms fired would be one a consumer could not reproduce.

ResultNamed, so a recipe can migrate before its item does or produce somebody else's item outright: probed through ItemExists, and the recipe name becomes required because there is no declared item to inherit one from.

EmitSettings and EmitData beside Emit, each refusing at the wrong stage family, so a settings-only guest no longer links the 21047-line data planner the pilot measured. HOST STUBS for all three in both languages, panicking with one shared message, which is what removes the -tags tinygo.wasm the pilot had to add to its make check: a guest is written once and vetted constantly, so the tag belongs on the build that produces the wasm.

PlanSettings narrowed to Named (Go interface, Rust supertrait), so a consumer's settings test implements one method instead of ten, and LocaleEntries / locale_entries exported so a consumer can ask the parser questions this library cannot, such as whether their hand-rolled entity has a name entry.

One cross-language divergence was found by the mirror rather than by review: Go's after_tech resolution was moved to the emitted name and Rust's was not, so a generated technology anchored on a LEGACY one produced a prefixed prerequisite in Rust and a verbatim one in Go. It failed the moment the mirrored test ran, which is the two-language design paying for itself.

One naming asymmetry, forced: the Go half exports Pair to build an Extra field, because the package already has an unexported kv that every prototype builder uses and Go will not carry two identifiers of one name. Rust exports its kv directly.

Two hardening items from the confirmations. First, Extra was the one spec slice not deep-copied: copyKVs snapshotted the KV backbone, but a Value carries Arr and Map as SLICE HEADERS, so a Pair holding a container left the plan pointing at the caller's backing array and a list built up before the declaration could rewrite it after. Fixed with a recursive copyValue, red-proven on all three specs across both container arms and two nesting levels. The Rust mirror needs none of it and that is BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by omission, verified rather than assumed: its spec is taken by value and moved into the declaration, and Vec<(String, Value)> owns its contents, so the caller has nothing left to mutate and the bug is unrepresentable. Second, ResultNamed naming an item the plan itself declares refused correctly with the wrong sentence, because the probe asks the World and the World is data.raw before this plan runs; it now has its own message naming the handle as the fix, checked ahead of the probe and compared on emitted names so a legacy declaration and a generated one are both covered.

## The consumer-round review

Both reviewers took the fixes for BetterBeltBalancer's blocks before the commit. What held, proven rather than asserted: the protoName seam is total (mutating it to prefix every name and re-running a plan with legacy names on both ends of every reference path, results, ingredients through IngredientsBy, unlocks, the AfterTech prerequisite, the splice value and the overwrite lookups all moved, the splice path correctly kept the world's name, zero bypasses); BBB's exact blocked shape emits three verbatim-named prototypes through the wire with the entity probe answering through the derived-entity walk on a cached env read, and the pilot's abort shape now refuses by name with zero extends; every wrong-family EmitSettings and EmitData call raises exactly once; the example guest vets and builds with no build tag; the packaged module still imports exactly eight host functions and exports nothing new; Extra's collision lists match what each emitter writes exactly (nothing double-emittable, nothing over-reserved), nested Extra values survive the conversion, and numeric map keys are unrepresentable inbound; the duplicate scan is per kind, as Factorio's namespaces are; and every new sentence is byte-identical across the halves over two new corpora. Two findings, both closed before the commit: Extra was the one spec slice snapshotted only one level deep in Go (a Pair holding a container aliased the plan after declaration; Rust moves its owned Vec so the aliasing is impossible by construction), now deep-copied with the asymmetry recorded beside the copy; and ResultNamed naming an item the plan itself declares refused with "does not exist" because the probe asks the World before the plan's own items are there, now refused with a sentence that points at the handle. Recorded from the implementation round as well: a rate-limit cut left the Rust library edits without their fixture updates, found by building the tests before continuing; and the mirror caught Rust's after_tech resolution still building prefix plus name where Go had moved to the seam, the two-language design paying for itself again.

## What v1 leaves open

From the design's own scope fences: no removal pre-check for content other mods depend on (Q5, a v2 feature with its own design); no arithmetic on count_formula (scaling a formula needs the MathExpression grammar); no control-stage half (a real consumer need ships as a separate package so the data half stays pin-free); no cached indexes (enumeration is linear on demand, measured affordable).

Asks for FkLua, from the dogfooding (the reason this repo exists): ALL FIVE ACTED ON UPSTREAM, and NO FINDINGS REMAIN OPEN ON FkLua'S SIDE AS OF THIS ROUND.

- A guest-facing raise import in the fkdata ABI. Shipped as `fkdata.Raise` / `fkdata::raise` and adopted at every diagnostic exit here; see the graded entry above for what the player reads before and after, and for the message reshape and the `World` trait consequence that adoption forced.
- The NaN advisory firing on library internals. Capped: the wall is now eight named entries and a `... and N more of the same class` tail, remedy kept. It still names this library's functions, which is inherent to naming the operation, but a consumer no longer reads 22 lines of it.
- `--report`'s pruning block reading like a problem for a data-only mod. Fixed as not-even-correct: `complete` is now true with `api.table_attached` false, so the data-only case reports as complete rather than as incomplete-but-fine.
- `fklua init --library` growing a data-stage flavor. Shipped as `--data`, and its contract text was better than this library's in two places, both adopted.
- The scaffolded go.mod's subdirectory-suffix trap. Closed upstream.

Open on THIS side, after the closing round:

CLOSED this round, each with mirrored red proofs:

- The locale checker's two-directional prefix limit. `CheckLocaleWith` / `check_locale_with` takes the consumer's complete set of settings declared outside this library, and with it the name and description orphan scan is complete-list rather than prefix-shaped: a renamed legacy setting's leftover entry is caught even carrying no prefix, and a same-prefix hand-rolled name is no longer a false positive. The list suppresses orphans without creating obligations (a name says nothing about what entries that setting needs), the value direction is unchanged for the same reason, and a name in both the list and the plan is a contradiction reported first in the checker's own voice. Plain `CheckLocale` keeps its documented prefix-only semantics: it never invents an orphan, it just cannot see every one.
- The engine gate's SKIPPED outcome under CI. `scripts/run-ingame.sh --strict`, also `FKRECIPES_STRICT=1`, makes an environmental mod-set skip exit 1 with the same both-sets-printed report and a verdict line saying why. The default is unchanged, which is FkLua's convention and the right one for a developer whose DLC differs; the strict form is for CI, where the mod set is fixed by the image and a skip means the gate quietly stopped running. Strict guards the CAPTURE as well as the comparison, and that second guard is the one worth naming: SKIPPED is set in the comparison branch and is structurally 0 in the capture branch, so a first cut guarded the reporting path and waved the writing path straight through. A wrong comparison reports; a wrong capture writes the wrong world into the file every later run is measured against, and the header invites a CI job to set FKRECIPES_STRICT=1 once for everything it runs. Under strict, recording over an existing line whose mod set differs now refuses and exits 1; plain --update still records any mod set, because re-recording after a deliberate environment change is what it is for.
- A min-only NumericSpec in the example goldens. `tempering-hold` is declared LAST in both examples (order "ag", so no existing order moved), with a minimum of 0.5 and no maximum, and bound as the salvage recipe's crafting time. The binding is what makes it say something beyond its own shape: a DECLARED minimum stands, and the generated floor-safe minimum fills in only where the consumer named none. Both goldens regenerated deliberately, and both gates carry a new assertion for it.
- The two-data-hook restriction, which was recorded more harshly than it is. The rule is one data-family hook PER PLAN, not per mod: two Libs may split creation (fk_data) from cross-mod patching (fk_data_updates), each emitting its own settings at fk_settings, as long as their names differ. Proven on the host in both languages by planning the patch against a world carrying the creating plan's prototypes, as data.raw actually looks a stage later, and asserting the patch plans cleanly and its splice rewrites the creating plan's technology. The paired test proves the overwrite refusal still fires when the two plans DO share a name, so the pair cannot both pass on a library that stopped refusing.

ALSO CLOSED, the two that were carried as deliberately-open one round earlier:

- CostBy's "exists" condition in the ladder is GONE rather than documented. It was kept as defence in depth on the argument that it named the intent, but that argument does not survive being stated plainly: it was a branch no test could make load-bearing, because a technology that does not exist carries no unit either and the following arm steps past both by the same test. Code that cannot go red is a liability wearing the costume of a defence, so it is deleted and the comment now says why absence is covered. The property is pinned by an explicit test that an absent rung is stepped past, red-proven in both languages by making the no-unit case yield an empty map instead of skipping: the ladder then settles on a technology the game does not have, priced at nothing. Holding that standard consistently then cost one more round, because the replacement arm had the same defect the deleted check did: Go's !ok term could not go red either, since every World this repository ships returns Nil beside false and the shape check covered absence on its own. The fixture now answers a real MAP beside ok=false for a named technology, which is a contract violation a consumer's own World can commit by accident, and each term has exactly one witness that only it turns red. The languages differ here for a reason worth recording rather than smoothing over: Go's TechUnit returns a (Value, bool) pair that can disagree with itself, so the flag and the shape are two guards; Rust's tech_unit returns Option<Value>, where a value cannot ride along with absence at all, so that case is unrepresentable and the single fall-through arm is the whole guard. The map-beside-absent test is Go-only by construction, not by omission, and both halves of Rust's one arm are witnessed separately.
- The stage dispatch. Emit's shape was "settings plans settings, EVERYTHING ELSE plans data", correct for the four stages fkdata names today and quietly wrong for any fifth: if FkLua ever wires settings-updates or settings-final-fixes, that shape runs PlanData at a settings stage where data.raw does not exist, and the failure surfaces as a confusing probe error rather than a sentence naming the cause. The decision now lives in the pure half as StageKindOf / stage_kind, mapping the four names it plans for and returning none for everything else including "unknown" and the two unwired ones, with Emit raising "fkrecipes: the stage <name> is not one this library plans for; route fk_settings and one data-family hook into Emit". The mapping is red-proven in both languages in both directions; the raise itself is behind the wasm gate, so it is covered by the pure mapping plus the existing end-to-end raise proofs rather than by a host test that cannot exist. Upstream wiring, if it happens, is now a one-line change here plus a decision about what the settings family should do.

NOTHING REMAINS OPEN ON THIS SIDE.

## The customizer round: the requirements gap, and the language that closed it

The commits: 3c5036e language, 8b1570e customize, ade4735 consumer, plus this report. The previous closing line ("nothing remains open on this side") is superseded by the last subsection below.

The pilot's second round was a pass (both hashes unmoved on both mod sets, the three prototypes byte-identical, the vet tag gone), and it exposed a gap in the charter rather than in the code: the library's stated value was a recipe customizer in the Mod Settings screen, and the charter as resolved bound only enablement, crafting time and (through the pilot's port) a dropdown over author-written presets. Nothing let a player write an ingredient list. The maintainer chose a mini language over per-ingredient slot settings and asked for the language to be designed in this session's own context, with a migration path from a shipped dropdown that preserves every stored preference. The design record is [customizer-design.md](customizer-design.md); this section is the report.

### What was measured before a word was designed

Every engine fact the language rests on was probed on Factorio 2.0.77 (build 84539) with a Lua-only mod under `--dump-data` and a private write-data directory, and is tabulated in the design record with its probe: the settings stage sees an empty `data.raw` (so no picker over the game's items can exist); setting prototypes take composed inline names and descriptions; prototype names are `A-Z a-z 0-9 _ -` at most 200 long; item amounts are 1 to 65535 and a fraction loads as written; a fluid amount must exceed 0 and a fluid in the default `crafting` category refuses; duplicates refuse; a research unit takes `tool` items only and an empty unit loads; a 2401-character default loads; and, through a property-tree writer built for the purpose, the `mod-settings.dat` round trip: a stored text is not trimmed, an out-of-list dropdown value and an out-of-range number reset to the default, a blank resets on a setting without `allow_blank`, ints read as i64 or double, and unknown keys survive. The design review measured more: a localised string takes 20 parameters and 20 levels; a fluid amount above about 1.07e301 aborts the engine rather than refusing; invalid UTF-8, NUL and control characters survive the file (a NUL then truncates inside the engine only when the whole stored value is 22 bytes or fewer, re-measured in fix round 1b); a wrong-typed stored value resets with a logged message; `1x2-remnants` and `loader-1x1` exist in base with an `x` between digits; `parameter-0` to `parameter-9` are both items and fluids; and, decisive for the migration path, the engine writes an UNTOUCHED default into `mod-settings.dat` exactly like an edited one.

### The language, and the review that changed it

The first specification was written, implemented in both halves against a 106-case corpus, and then reviewed adversarially as a design before the code hardened. The review's worst finding was measured: because untouched defaults are stored, "untouched means equal to the rendered default" would have turned every rebalance of a mod's default list into a hard load failure for players who never opened the settings, in any modpack lacking a ladder's first rung. The default text became the reserved word `default`. The same review changed the treatment of display names typed by habit (`2 iron plates` now suggests `iron-plate`), decimal commas, thousands separators, invisible pasted characters (named by code point), invalid UTF-8 (refused whole, identically in both halves, which at the time decoded the wire differently; sync pass 1 below closes that), the fluid ceiling, a `custom` preset a mod already ships (`CustomValue`), the `x` inside a prototype name, the unpinned bracket-aware comma split, a rendering rule that was not closed under the parser, pack ladders, a 2000-character bound, and the composition of a dropdown's description with its presets under their localised labels. The corpus grew to 203 cases with an escape syntax for raw bytes and code points, and both halves were revised to it.

Two code reviews then ran, one per half, each with its own differential fuzz across the two parsers (105,762 and 69,999 inputs). They found the two halves aligned in opposite directions on two rules the first corpus had left open (each implementer had read the other's intermediate tree), a trailing comma stripped before the bracket-aware split on one side, and the fluid amount rendered with each platform's own tie-break: Go's shortest-round-trip printer and Rust's `Display` disagree in the last digit on 129 of 204,105 doubles, the smallest at 1.0000076293945312. This repository's transcript formatter had met that once already. The renderer now takes the fewest correctly rounded significant digits that read back to the same double and breaks an exact tie towards the even digit itself, and the corpus pins two exact ties. Every finding closed before the language commit (3c5036e).

### The binding, the harness, and what only the harness found

The customize commit (8b1570e) bound the language: text settings with the `default` word, `IngredientsFrom`, `CostFrom` with a `CustomCost`, the Custom arm on both dropdown kinds, the composed descriptions nested at the engine's 20-parameter limit, the locale checker's two new rules, and the semantics both halves share, written into the design record as a contract of orderings before either half was reviewed. The two reviews' differentials over the example guests' emitted streams (2,346 and more than 50,000 lines) found no divergence; what they found was a Rust settings-stage panic on an unvalidated handle where Go refused, a non-finite research count that trapped in Go and rendered `NaN` in Rust, a design over-reach (a dropdown whose presets already cover `custom` must stay legal without an arm), and a rule neither half had (two recipes arming one dropdown). The harness agent grew the mirror stand-in to the measured prototype rules, re-recorded the transcript at 46 lines, built a host-only codec for `mod-settings.dat` (measured against the install's own file: 196 bytes decoded and re-encoded identically) and gave the in-game gate a flipped row driven by it, so each dropdown is on `custom` in one gate and on a preset in the other. The harness also found the one defect neither review reached, under a real engine: a text could not name the plan's own items, because they are extended after the text is read, while the composed description showed exactly those names. The resolver now sees the plan's own emitted item names.

### The pilot's second round, folded in

The consumer commit (ade4735) lets a recipe carry `enabled` in `Extra` when nothing in the plan unlocks it, emitting the consumer's value in the library's own slot so the pilot's field order holds, and refusing by the technology's name when a plan technology does unlock it; the docs now say the place_result probe runs inside `Emit` and name the `max_level` inheritance a migrating mod meets. The place_result refusal names the item and the entity (`fkrecipes: the item balancer-part names a place_result bbb-balancer-1-to-2 that does not exist`).

Size, measured with a freshly built `fklua mod --report` against both packaged examples at the language commit (3c5036e) and at the customize head, the Go guest built with tinygo as run-mirror.sh builds it and the Rust guest with `cargo build --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown -p datastage`:

| | fk_data_module.lua lines | wasm bytes | packaged Lua bytes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Go at 3c5036e | 54362 | 513748 | 2218169 |
| Go at the customize head | 100966 | 1006402 | 3915886 |
| Rust at 3c5036e | 48607 | 108764 | 2020580 |
| Rust at the customize head | 89818 | 224815 | 3648199 |

The customizer roughly doubles the Go data module, and that is the round's real cost. `fklua mod --report` printed no headroom figure at the time: FkLua's limit lived in `internal/luagen/funclimit.go` as `maxJumpSpanBytes = 655355`, enforced and never reported, and the relay was already carrying both example modules. Any figure read off the packaged text was the relay's hop rather than headroom: the span left after the relay is about half the limit whatever the plan's size, which is why a first reading of it put every module at 50 percent and the pilot's own build at 83 percent of a number that turned out to be the hop. The ceiling this library will meet is the relay's precondition (a station needs body-level room inside the oversized function), not the offset itself. Two FkLua asks followed: report the pre-relay span and the station count in `--report`, and state the relay's precondition in the data-stage documentation. Both are RESOLVED (the sync pass 1 ledger below): since that pass both gate scripts print the `jumps` row out of the report, and nothing here measures the span any other way.
