### What this round leaves open on this side

The claims a headless probe cannot settle are carried as assumptions in the design record and handed to the pilot's third round, which runs a client: that `\n` renders as a line break in a setting's description tooltip; that a composed multi-line description is readable there; whether the text field has a length limit of its own or renders rich text; whether shift-clicking an item inserts a tag there (the sentence claiming it was removed from the reference); whether a research with no packs completes. Two deliberate limits: one text setting serves one recipe, and a dropdown armed by a recipe and a technology at once is accepted with the technology's description winning. Growth is bounded by decisions rather than by luck: a future modifier is sigil-introduced, `-` is not available as a range separator, and `*` stays a multiplication sign.

And the size trajectory above: the customizer is worth its cost only if the modules stay loadable, and the number that says whether they do is not reported today. That is the first thing the next FkLua round should measure and print.

### Follow-up, part one: the items the FkLua and BetterBeltBalancer rounds handed back

This subsection is the report for the follow-up commits after c7a806e, one per item. Part two, the items that needed FkLua's new head on the distribution channel (fkdata's byte-string surface, retiring `go/internal/modsettings` for `fklua modsettings write`, and reading the `jumps` object in the harness), landed once that head was pushed and is reported under "Sync pass 1" below. Every number below carries the command that produced it; the FkLua binary in the commands is built from the checkout's branch named beside it into a scratch directory, and nothing is written into the checkout.

**The measured corrections (item 4).** Two rows of the design record and the relay paragraph above were corrected against FkLua's data-stage probe (its `agents/datastage.md`, r5-landing round): the localised-string nesting limit is 19 tables (RE-MEASURED in fix round 2 below as 20 LEVELS: that probe row counted tables where the engine counts depth, and this reading was wrong), the fluid ceiling is DBL_MAX / 2^24 = 1.0715086071862672e301 bracketed at 1.0715e301 and 1.0716e301, and the relay figures recorded here were scanned over the post-relay text. The report's `jumps` object, read for the first time from a scratch build of FkLua's r5-landing head (9591650) over the two example modules as run-mirror.sh builds them at this commit:

```sh
cd /path/to/FkLua && git archive r5-landing | tar -x -C "$SCRATCH/fklua-r5" && (cd "$SCRATCH/fklua-r5" && go build -o "$SCRATCH/bin/fklua-r5" ./cmd/fklua)
cd go/examples/datastage && tinygo build -target=wasm-unknown -scheduler=none -gc=leaking -opt=2 -o "$SCRATCH/go.wasm" .
cd rust && cargo build --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown -p datastage   # target/wasm32-unknown-unknown/release/datastage.wasm
"$SCRATCH/bin/fklua-r5" mod --data-module "$SCRATCH/go.wasm" --name fkrecipes-example --version 0.1.0 --author Techrocket9 --factorio-version 2.0 -o "$SCRATCH/mods-go" --report "$SCRATCH/report-go.json"
jq -c .jumps.data "$SCRATCH/report-go.json"
```

| module (at c7a806e, packaged by 9591650) | widest span, function | stations | span after the relay | widest block | block room |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Go example | 954,969 bytes in `(*Lib).PlanData`, 146% of the 655,355 limit | 11 | 328,189 (50%) | 802 | 326,875 (100%) |
| Rust example | 559,026 bytes in `Lib::plan_data`, 85% | 0, no relay | 559,026 | 461 | 327,216 (100%) |

Those are smaller than the figures the previous round scanned because r5-landing's luagen round took about ten percent off the Go module and took the Rust module out of the relay altogether (FkLua's `agents/codegen.md`); the numbers this repository steers by from here are the ones that report prints, and part two wires them into both harness scripts. Read again at b88965d: the widest-block and block-room columns of that table are the relayed function's own figures (`relayed_functions[0]` in the object), not the module's, because b88965d made the module row the module's worst run; the sync pass 1 subsection carries the module figures.

**The language links only when a text setting is declared (item 1).** The pilot's finding was that a plan with no text setting still shipped parseIngredientList, classifyPiece and resolveCustomCost, because `PlanData` and `PlanSettings` called the parser, the renderer, the amount formatter and the custom-cost resolver by name and whole-program elimination has to keep what is named. Both halves now reach them only through values the two text-setting constructors install on the plan: Go holds a `language` struct of function values (`lang`) and a `customCost` function value on `Lib`, installed by `ingredientsSetting` (the language) and `packsSetting` (the language and the resolver); Rust holds `Option<&'static Language>` over a `static LANGUAGE` vtable of fn pointers in `ingredient_list.rs` and `Option<CustomCostFn>`, installed by `ingredients_decl` and `packs_decl` the same way. They are plan-level rather than package-level because consumers run parallel tests and `go test -race` is a gate. A text setting met by a planner with nothing installed is unreachable through the public surface and is refused anyway, with one sentence in both halves (`fkrecipes: the text setting <name> was declared without the ingredient language; declare it through IngredientsSetting or PacksSetting`, the Go names on purpose), witnessed by internal tests that append a declaration by hand; the handle validators for the two text kinds now also check the setting's KIND, because with the seam a right-typed handle at a dropdown's index was a nil function call where it used to be a wrong read. Two adversarial reviews, one per half, each with a differential over HEAD and the change (Go: 19 plans and 7 Worlds, 498 transcript lines; Rust: 13 worlds and 12 refusing plans, 370 lines) found no behavioural drift and the mirror and in-game goldens did not move. What they found instead was that the seam's tripwire was weaker than the seam: Rust's source-text property missed the path form `Lib::resolve_custom_cost(self, ..)` (injected, suite green, the no-text module regrew 3.3 percent), had no stale-table floor, and had a scanner that a `'"'` char literal blinded for the rest of the file; Go's missed a parenthesised callee, a function value taken in an allowed file and called through an unnamed expression (87,364 bytes regrown with the suite green), and 40 of the 45 top-level functions of ingredientlist.go (a `splitEntries` call from the planner, 54,204 bytes regrown). Both properties are now derived from the language file itself: Go guards every top-level function of ingredientlist.go plus resolveCustomCost and allows a name only inside the two installing constructors and the resolver's own definition; Rust guards a table of nine rows and adds the rule that every `pub(crate)` fn or static of ingredient_list.rs must be a guarded name, so widening a private function's visibility is a red suite. Each hole above is a red proof in the commit.

The fixtures are `go/examples/notext` and `rust/examples/notext`, the pilot's pre-customizer shape written twice; the measurement, with the same fklua (built from FkLua master at a1fcd04 into a scratch directory) and the harness's build flags, before (c7a806e) and after this commit:

```sh
cd go/examples/notext && tinygo build -target=wasm-unknown -scheduler=none -gc=leaking -opt=2 -o "$SCRATCH/notext.wasm" .
cd rust && cargo build --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown -p notext        # target/wasm32-unknown-unknown/release/notext.wasm
"$SCRATCH/bin/fklua" mod --data-module "$SCRATCH/notext.wasm" --name better-belt-balancer --version 0.3.2 --author Techrocket9 --factorio-version 2.0 -o "$SCRATCH/mods-notext" --report "$SCRATCH/report-notext.json"
F="$SCRATCH/mods-notext/better-belt-balancer_0.3.2/fk_data_module.lua"; wc -c -l "$F"
for n in parseIngredientList classifyPiece resolveCustomCost renderIngredientList formatListAmount; do grep -c "$n" "$F"; done   # the Go module
grep -c '^-- .*ingredient_list' "$F"; grep -c resolve_custom_cost "$F"; grep -c '^-- .*classify' "$F"                            # the Rust module
```

| guest | wasm bytes, before and after | fk_data_module.lua bytes, before and after | lines, before and after |
|---|---|---|---|
| go/examples/notext (no text setting) | 982,781 to 640,000 | 3,658,810 to 2,716,663 (25.7 percent gone) | 95,227 to 67,023 |
| rust/examples/notext (no text setting) | 214,120 to 134,036 | 3,416,320 to 2,475,085 (27.5 percent gone) | 84,959 to 59,491 |
| go/examples/datastage (declares text settings) | 1,006,742 to 996,001 | 3,916,145 to 3,953,233 (0.95 percent added) | 100,966 to 101,579 |
| rust/examples/datastage (declares text settings) | 225,246 to 226,529 | 3,656,235 to 3,679,378 (0.63 percent added) | 89,943 to 90,520 |

THIS TABLE IS THE SEAM'S OWN BEFORE AND AFTER, at c7a806e and against an `fklua` at a1fcd04, and no column of it is a figure for the current tree. The CURRENT pair for all four guests, with a recipe that runs as printed beside them, is the four-fixture table under "The claim gets its adjective" in Fix round 1b below; that is where a comment saying "compare the counts against the pinned figures" now sends a maintainer, and `go/examples/notext/main.go`, `go/lib.go` and `rust/src/ingredient_list.rs` say so.

The Rust before row was taken on the same source as an out-of-tree crate reaching `rust/` by an absolute path, which rustc writes into the wasm; as the member it is 2,475,085 bytes where the out-of-tree build was 2,475,631, same 59,491 lines. The function headers: the Go no-text module carried one header each for parseIngredientList, classifyPiece, resolveCustomCost, renderIngredientList and formatListAmount and now carries none of them and none of the other 40 functions of ingredientlist.go; the Rust one carried 23 headers naming `ingredient_list`, one `resolve_custom_cost` and two `classify`, and now carries 0, 0 and 3 headers naming `ingredient_list`, all three glue over the language's data types (`RawVec<ListEntry>::grow_one`, `Vec<ListEntry>` as Drop, `drop_glue<ListText>`), which the settings planner still handles by value. The example guests keep every header, which is the seam working the other way. The line and byte counts are the oracle and the greps are a secondary signal: TinyGo inlines a single-caller function and its header leaves the module while its code stays, which is how both review leaks above sat behind greps at zero. Read through the r5-landing report, the widest span moves with the seam by under one percent: the Go example 954,969 to 962,558 bytes in PlanData (11 stations both), the Go fixture 954,969 to 966,557, the Rust example 559,026 to 561,174 with no relay; block room stays at 100 percent on all three.

**Building the Go example without DWARF (item 5): measured, and the flag is not taken.** The premise had two halves: that three quarters of the Go example's wasm is debug sections the emitter never reads, and that the packaged Lua is therefore unchanged by tinygo's `-no-debug`. The first half is true. At c7a806e the example builds to 1,006,742 bytes with the harness's flags and 227,286 with `-no-debug` added (77.4 percent DWARF); at 3601004, 996,001 and 228,773. The second half is false, and the proof asked for is the one that fails: `cmp` on the two packaged modules differs at byte 210,862 (line 2718), the stripped module is 3,889,044 bytes / 99,961 lines against 3,916,145 / 100,966, and 115,614 of the differing lines are code rather than comments, because the function table is renumbered (`TBL[1] = 60` becomes `57`) after TinyGo INLINES 24 functions it keeps as functions when debug information is present: `(*Lib).PlanData`, `Emit`, `checkCycles`, `unlockedRecipes`, `unlockingTech`, `main.plan`, both `#wasmexport` wrappers, `resolveName`, `entryProblem`, the `UnimplementedWorld` methods and two World interface thunks, `runtime.printitf`, `runtime.panicOrGoexit` and `__wasm_call_ctors` among them (the `-- <name> (<sig>)` headers of the two modules, `comm -23`). The emitter does read debug information for one output, `fk_data_module.map.json`, which carries `src` and `line` per function and shrinks from 42,892 to 22,499 bytes. Read through the r5-landing report, the stripped module's widest span is 1,281,678 bytes in `main.onSettings#wasmexport` (196 percent of the limit, 40 stations) where the debug build's is 954,969 in `PlanData` (146 percent, 11 stations); the span after the relay is 328,876 against 328,189, the widest block 807 against 802, and the block room 326,870 against 326,875 (100 percent either way). Behaviour does not move: with `-no-debug` added to both scripts' tinygo lines, run-mirror.sh's Go transcript is byte-identical to the golden and run-ingame.sh's two rows hash to the golden on 2.0.77 (`e824cd83...`/`72d8ca91...` default, `b5ce9d7e...`/`72d8ca91...` flipped). The pilot builds its data module with exactly the harness's flags (BetterBeltBalancer's Makefile, the `$(DATA_WASM)` rule) and so does every FkLua script, so the harness keeps DWARF: the two gates exist to package what a consumer packages, and the widest span part two will print and gate has to be the consumer's function, not one TinyGo assembles differently when a flag the consumer does not pass is present. The wasm column of the size tables above is therefore three quarters DWARF, the README says so beside its wasm figures, and the flag is one word in two scripts if the operator prefers the smaller intermediate.

**Where a generated setting lands (items 7a and 7b).** The pilot's finding was that a generated setting's two-letter order comes from its declaration index alone, so beside legacy orders `a` and `b` its research customizer's three fields sorted above the dropdown that switches them on, and no ordering of the declarations could fix it; the pilot declared all four new settings Legacy solely to place them. The surface chosen is one method, `OrderAfter(order)` / `order_after`, and not a per-setting order parameter (which would be six more constructors, one per kind) nor a change to the two letters (which would move every consumer's orders and both goldens): every generated setting declared after the call captures the named order as a prefix and emits it followed by the two letters it already had, so it keeps its generated name, sorts after the legacy setting with that order and before every legacy order that sorts after that one, and a plan that never calls it emits exactly what it did. The prefix is captured at declaration rather than read at plan time, so a call places what follows it and a second call moves on. Three refusals, all at the settings stage, both halves byte-identical: an empty order (`fkrecipes: OrderAfter was given an empty order; name the order string the generated settings should follow`, the Go name in both halves as the language guard's sentence is), a generated order equal to a legacy one whether or not the method was called (`... would carry the order <O>, which the legacy setting <j> already carries; give one of them an order of its own`; this is the one behaviour change for a plan that never calls the method: a tie that loaded with the two in the engine's hands is refused now, and migration.md says so), and a placed setting that would sort past a legacy order extending the one it was placed after (`... would carry the order <O> and sort past the legacy setting <j> at <L>, which extends <P>; OrderAfter(<P>) places settings before every legacy order that extends <P>`), which is the misplacement the method exists to remove one level down: beside legacy `a` and `ab`, twenty-four generated settings fit under `a` and the twenty-fifth reaches `aba`; beside `b` and `ba` nothing fits under `b`, and the remedy is to name `ba`. The rule is exactly complete for the promise (any legacy order between the prefix and the emitted order has the prefix as a proper prefix), and two generated orders cannot collide below the 676 wrap (two letters always, so equal strings force equal prefixes). The order checks run as a second pass after the per-setting loop, because the first draft ran them inside it and the reviews showed it quoting a legacy setting whose empty name had not been checked yet (a doubled space where the name belongs) and pre-empting five refusals the engine would fail the load on. The same reviews retired a false claim from the doc comment (a placed setting does not sort before every order that fails to extend the named one; it sorts after the named one, and orders that sort before it are unaffected), an engine claim nobody measured in the tie sentence (that the settings screen breaks a tie by name), and a miscount that lived in both halves and both guides (the two letters count declaration slots, legacy ones included, so beside `a` and `b` it is the twenty-seventh declaration that carries `ba`). Parity: a 1,014-plan sweep (26 legacy orders, three setting counts, thirteen prefixes including non-ASCII) and a 37-plan hand battery produced byte-identical transcripts and refusals in both halves; a HEAD differential over plans that never call the method differs only in the tie refusal. Nothing in the example guests calls the method (they declare no legacy settings), so neither golden moved and the engine has not yet loaded a three-letter order from this library; the pilot's next round, which mixes legacy and generated settings, is where it meets the engine. docs/migration.md's worked example now shows the pilot's four customizer settings placed with the method under generated names, and states what the pilot shipped instead (the Legacy forms with hand-written `bbb-` names and orders) and why; docs/usage.md's sentence about declaration order is replaced by the rule for a mixed plan.

**The two texts a Custom-armed cost dropdown produces (items 7c and 7d).** The pilot found that an edited research count or seconds under a tier drew no line where the edited pack text drew one, and that the cost dropdown's composed description read `cost of logistics-2`, a raw key beside a localised label. Both halves now log one line per edited setting under a tier, in the order count, seconds, packs and before the tier's own lines (`fkrecipes: <setting> is edited, but <dropdown> is not on <custom>, so the number is ignored`; edited means readable, a number, and not the declared default, and a stored NaN counts as an edit on purpose while the same NaN on the custom arm refuses), and compose a tier's line as `{"", "\n", {"string-mod-setting.<setting>-<value>"}, ": cost of ", {"technology-name.<first source>"}}`, the technology named through its locale key beside the label. The review of that shape corrected two claims before they landed: the technology-name table is a sibling of the label (a one-preset description is three tables deep before and after, measured), and a missing technology or entry renders the engine's `Unknown key:` marker (this repository's own measured note in locale.go), which reads worse than the bare name the line used to print; that is the price of the localised name and the consumer's ladder to order. **BOTH HALVES OF THAT SENTENCE ARE SUPERSEDED, and the shape above with them, by fix round 2's decision D**, which is where the current statements live: the line's key tables are wrapped as `{"?", {key}, "raw"}`; a one-preset cost description is FOUR tables deep, the three this round measured plus the one level every wrapper spends; and a missing technology or entry does NOT render the `Unknown key:` marker on a setting, it deletes the info icon and the entire tooltip, silently and with exit 0, which is why the wrapper exists. The corrected figures are re-measured out of the engine's own settings dump in `agents/customizer-design.md`'s client-round section. This paragraph is left as the record of what that day's review decided. The same review found that the ignored line would lie whenever the count or seconds setting is read somewhere live, which nothing forbade (one double could back a recipe's `CraftTimeFrom` and a technology's custom cost, or two technologies could share a count), so the text settings' exactly-once rule now has a numeric twin at the end of `validateBindings`: `fkrecipes: the setting <name> is read as a research count or time by more than one declaration; a custom cost's number serves exactly one`, with two recipes still free to share a crafting time. And it found a pre-existing parity defect the previous round had written down as agreed and one half had not followed: on the custom arm Rust refused a bad count or seconds before parsing the pack text while Go parses the text first (136 of an 854-case battery diverged, identically at HEAD); Rust now follows the record. The mirror stand-in flips `tips-count` to 45 under `tips-research-tier` on military with the seconds and the pack text untouched, so the transcript golden gains exactly one ignored-number line and the harness asserts that the untouched two draw none; the composed cost lines change in the settings-stage extend and in the final dump; the in-game settings hash moves on both rows (the description is a prototype field) from `72d8ca91...` to `2b44da19...` and the data hashes do not, and run-ingame.sh asserts the nested `technology-name.military-4` key in the engine's own settings dump. Two more witnesses came out of the reviews: a fixture World that answers a value beside ok=false for a startup setting (Go), so the `!ok` term of both ignored-line functions has a witness, and a cost-dropdown nesting test past nineteen presets in both halves. Left as notes: `CostChoice.Sources` entries are not validated (an empty rung composes `{"technology-name."}`), and the localised nesting rule outruns the engine's 19-table ceiling past about 343 presets in either kind of dropdown, both pre-existing and unchanged.

```sh
cd go/examples/datastage && tinygo build -target=wasm-unknown -scheduler=none -gc=leaking -opt=2 -o "$SCRATCH/go-debug.wasm" . && tinygo build -target=wasm-unknown -scheduler=none -gc=leaking -opt=2 -no-debug -o "$SCRATCH/go-nodebug.wasm" .
for v in debug nodebug; do "$SCRATCH/bin/fklua" mod --data-module "$SCRATCH/go-$v.wasm" --name fkrecipes-example --version 0.1.0 --author Techrocket9 --factorio-version 2.0 -o "$SCRATCH/mods-go-$v" --report "$SCRATCH/report-go-$v.json"; done
cmp "$SCRATCH"/mods-go-{debug,nodebug}/fkrecipes-example_0.1.0/fk_data_module.lua; wc -c -l "$SCRATCH"/mods-go-*/fkrecipes-example_0.1.0/fk_data_module.lua
for v in debug nodebug; do grep -o '^-- [^ ]* (' "$SCRATCH/mods-go-$v/fkrecipes-example_0.1.0/fk_data_module.lua" | sort > "$SCRATCH/names-$v"; done; comm -23 "$SCRATCH"/names-{debug,nodebug}
"$SCRATCH/bin/fklua-r5" mod --data-module "$SCRATCH/go-nodebug.wasm" ... --report "$SCRATCH/r5-report-go-nodebug.json"; jq -c .jumps.data "$SCRATCH/r5-report-go-nodebug.json"
sed 's/-gc=leaking -opt=2 \\/-gc=leaking -opt=2 -no-debug \\/' scripts/run-mirror.sh > scripts/run-mirror-nodebug.sh && scripts/run-mirror-nodebug.sh   # and the same for run-ingame.sh; both deleted after
```

## Sync pass 1: FkRecipes on FkLua master b88965d

The pass that puts this library on the FkLua head the follow-up round waited for, through the real channel and nothing else, and lands the three follow-up items that needed it (the byte-string surface, retiring the in-house settings codec, reading the `jumps` object). One commit per item; every number carries its command; the FkLua checkout at /path/to/FkLua is read-only reference and the harness builds fklua from it into tmp/.

**The channel, first.** `git ls-remote https://github.com/Techrocket9/fklua refs/heads/master` answers b88965d0875de1771babb0c8fc86c7f767c5a935, which is the checkout's master and the head every number below was taken against. The Go require stays at `github.com/Techrocket9/fklua/guest/go v0.2.0` in both Go modules: the guest/go tags are v0.1.0 and v0.2.0 only (`git tag -l 'guest/go/*'`), this repository requires a tag and never a pseudo-version or a replace, and `git diff guest/go/v0.2.0 master -- guest/go/fkdata/fkdata.go` in the checkout is 45 insertions and 4 deletions of which every line is a doc comment except a gofmt realignment of two struct fields (`Str string` / `Arr []V`), so the Go guest's behaviour did not move and go.sum is untouched. The cargo pin moved with `cd rust && cargo update -p fkdata -p fk`, ac0ebcc to b88965d, both `source` lines in Cargo.lock ending in the full hash; Cargo.toml still carries the git source and the [patch] one-source note and no live patch or path. The harness builds fklua from FKLUA_CHECKOUT at the same head, so the library dependency and the tool that packages it ride one commit again, the arrangement the alignment round chose.

**The byte-string surface (follow-up item 2).** After the pin moved, `cargo build --target wasm32-unknown-unknown -p fkrecipes` produced exactly six errors, all E0308, at exactly the six lines FkLua's landing agent had predicted in rust/src/emit.rs: 220 and 236 (`keys` now returns `Vec<LuaStr>`), 355 and 360 (`V::Str` takes a `LuaStr`), 386 (`V::Str(s)` yields one), 396 (a map key's `as_str()` is `Option<&str>`). The adaptation is a design decision rather than six `.into()` calls, because the pure Rust value model's `Value::Str(String)` cannot hold what fkdata now delivers byte-exact, and the two ways of making it fit (decode lossily in `from_v`, or let a non-UTF-8 string become the Nil marker) would each have reopened the design record's "both halves see the same refusal" decision on a path the engine can reach (invalid UTF-8 survives mod-settings.dat, measured in the customizer round). So the model gains one arm, `Value::Bytes(Vec<u8>)`, built only at the host boundary (`from_v`, when `LuaStr::as_str` is None) and by fixtures, written back byte-exact by `to_v` through `fkdata::bytes_`, and `#[non_exhaustive]` from the start with the same additive-growth note the `World` trait carries (Go's `Kind` switch already needs a default, so this is the Rust spelling of what a Go consumer already has). The language's `parse` and `is_edited` take `&[u8]`, and `parse` opens with `core::str::from_utf8`, the exact mirror of Go's `utf8.ValidString`; `read_text_setting` yields bytes from either arm; `note_ignored_text` counts a Bytes value as an edit, which is what Go's isEdited over the same bytes answers; `read_dropdown` refuses a Bytes value with the same holds-sentence as any unoffered value (an offered value is the author's own text, so no offered value can be those bytes; the rendering is only quoted, never compared, because a lossy rendering fed into the comparison could match an offered value the engine never stored); a number read treats Bytes as unreadable, Go's `Kind != KindNum`. Three places the Rust half now refuses where Go carries, all named in the code and all outside what the engine delivers (a prototype name is `A-Z a-z 0-9 _ -` by the engine's own refusal, measured in the design record): a technology name from `keys()` that is not UTF-8, a prerequisite string that is not, both through `fkdata::raise` with the sentence `value::not_text` builds (the surface named, the bytes in hex, fkdata's own precedent for its engine-constrained conveniences; the sentence lives in the pure half so a host test pins it), and a map KEY that is not UTF-8, which sinks the whole map to the Nil marker the way a numeric key does (a copied unit with one at its top level refuses as "not a dictionary", one nested refuses through the dropped-subtree check; both traced). `not_text` and its hex helper are `#[cfg(any(target_family = "wasm", test))]` rather than `allow(dead_code)`, measured to matter: with the witness test deleted, `RUSTFLAGS=-Dwarnings cargo clippy --lib --all-targets` errors with `function 'not_text' is never used` under the cfg and finishes clean under the attribute, so a deleted caller is a build error rather than a silenced warning, and that clippy form is a gate row now.

**The U+FFFD decision, taken once for both halves: dropped.** The clause that refused a literal replacement character (`strings.Contains(text, string(utf8.RuneError))` in Go, `text.contains('\u{fffd}')` in Rust) existed for one reason, stated in both comments: it was what the Rust reader put where invalid bytes had been, so refusing it was the only way the halves could agree about a hand-edited file. With both readers feeding the parser the bytes, that reason is gone, and a real U+FFFD a player pasted is one more character the ordinary rules refuse by quoting it (the names rule in an entry, the tag rule inside `[item=...]`, an earlier problem where there is one; the Go reviewer measured 2,457 such inputs and all three shapes, and the comments say "the ordinary character rules" for that reason). The corpus is the contract and changed first: line 189 (`2 iron-\uFFFDplate`) now expects `entry 1 ("2 iron-\uFFFDplate"): "\uFFFD" has no place here; names use ...`, and a new line 192 pins the whole-text `\uFFFD` case the Go table used to hold alone; both suites went red on 189 with the old clause (`got: contains characters that are not text`), which is the change's proof in both languages, and the three raw-byte cases (183, 486, 489) stay refused as not text by the surviving guard, red-proven in both halves by removing it (Go: `!utf8.ValidString` to `false` moves those three plus the not-text table and the order test; Rust: `from_utf8` to `from_utf8_lossy` moves the same three plus the boundary test). 204 cases, 71 accepted and 133 refused (`go test -run TestIngredientListCorpus -v`). The Rust corpus reader now returns raw bytes (`unescape` to `Vec<u8>`), so the `\xNN` escape reaches the library's own check rather than the reader's conversion, and its failure report escapes bytes outside printable ASCII as `\xNN`, so a raw-byte case and a literal-U+FFFD case print differently (they did not under the lossy rendering). The design record's decision-table row is rewritten to this contract.

**What the reviews found, and the one thing the differential found that nobody was looking for.** Two adversarial reviews, one per half, plus a cross-language differential through the PUBLIC surfaces of both halves (throwaway drivers in a scratch directory, one Go module by replace and one cargo crate by path, declaring the same plan: a text-fed recipe, a dropdown with a Custom arm, a research priced by a CustomCost; the Go World answering `Str` over the bytes and the Rust World answering `Str` or `Bytes` exactly as `from_v` does): 273,027 driven inputs over five targets (29,790 byte-mutation inputs at the text, packs, custom-text and number targets; 51,133 accepting-path inputs at three targets; 468 at the dropdown), 28,184 of them taking the not-text refusal, with ZERO differing lines everywhere except the dropdown target, where all 404 differences are the one designed divergence below and the refuse-or-accept decision agrees on every input. The eight Rust red proofs and the two Go ones were re-run by the reviewers and went red as reported. What the differential found first was that the Rust `World` trait was SEALED BY ACCIDENT: `pub use world::{stage_kind, StageKind, World}` re-exported the trait without its supertrait `Named`, `mod world` is private, and rustc refuses an outside `impl World` with "`World` is a sealed trait, because to implement it you also need to implement `fkrecipes::world::Named`, which is not accessible", while docs/usage.md promised a fixture World of your own works in Rust. Pre-existing since the seam was narrowed to Named, unseen because every Rust fixture in this repository lives inside the crate. Fixed by re-exporting `Named`, with a witness that could not have existed before: rust/tests/fixture_world.rs, an integration test and therefore a separate crate that sees only what a consumer sees, implementing both traits on a fixture of its own and driving `plan_data` twice (a Bytes text refused as not text; a list carried into the Op stream); red-proven by removing `Named` from the re-export (E0277, the sealed-trait note). usage.md now says a Rust fixture implements `Named` beside `World`. Four new Rust tests state Go's behaviour for the same bytes in their comments without a Go twin; each claim was verified against the Go source by the reviewer, and Go's behaviour is unchanged by construction (a Go string already carried the bytes), which is why no Go test was added.

**The one mirror gap this leaves, recorded here because no gate can see it.** The unoffered-dropdown refusal over a stored value that is not text: Go quotes the raw bytes (`holds "\x80preset"`) and Rust quotes them through `from_utf8_lossy` (`holds "\xef\xbf\xbdpreset"`, one U+FFFD per maximal invalid subpart), because `fkdata::raise` takes a `&str` and a refusal is text on that side. Unifying it would mean a hex rendering in both halves for a case the engine never delivers (an out-of-list stored dropdown value is reset to the default before any stage runs, measured in the design record), so it is a hand-edited-file guard on both sides and the divergence is in the quoting alone; the design record's row says so. The mirror stand-in holds no invalid bytes, so run-mirror.sh and run-ingame.sh are non-regression results for this change rather than witnesses; the corpus and the differential are the witnesses.

**Gates at the byte-string commit**, exit codes read directly: `cd go && gofmt -l .` prints nothing (0), `go vet ./...` 0, `go test ./...` 0, `go test -race ./...` 0, `cd go/examples/notext && go vet .` 0, the example's tinygo build with the harness's flags 0 (1,019,763 bytes of wasm, `wc -c`); `cd rust && cargo fmt --check` 0, `cargo test` 0 (175 unit tests and 2 integration tests), `RUSTFLAGS=-Dwarnings cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets` 0, `RUSTFLAGS=-Dwarnings cargo build` 0, `cargo build --target wasm32-unknown-unknown --workspace` 0; `scripts/run-mirror.sh` prints run-mirror: OK (0) with testdata/mirror/transcript.golden unmoved; `scripts/run-ingame.sh` prints run-ingame: OK (0) on Factorio 2.0.77 (build 84539, mac-arm64, steam; re-asked with `"$FACTORIO" --version`), both rows matching the committed golden (default e824cd83.../2b44da19..., flipped b5ce9d7e.../2b44da19..., mod set base, core, elevated-rails, quality, space-age); the docs-style greps over docs/usage.md and the repository dash grep print nothing (the grep excludes agents/docs-style.md, which quotes the characters it bans). The emitted stream does not depend on how strings cross, and neither golden moved.

**The mod-settings.dat codec is the toolchain's now (follow-up item 3, the retire commit).** `fklua modsettings write --from FILE.json --out mod-settings.dat` and `fklua modsettings read FILE` landed upstream at c21ff07 and were hardened at f80f61b, they read the identical JSON shape `testdata/ingame/flipped.json` already used, and scripts/run-ingame.sh builds an fklua from FKLUA_CHECKOUT on every run anyway, so `go/internal/modsettings` was a second implementation of a format with exactly one writer. It is deleted: the package, its two suites and cmd/writesettings, 1069 lines over five files (`git diff --cached --stat` at this commit). Nothing the library ships imported it, so pin transparency never depended on it, and `cd go && go build ./... && go vet ./... && go test ./...` are all 0 with it gone. The gate's writer step is now one line, `"$FKLUA" modsettings write --from "$FLIPPED_JSON" --out "$FLIPPED_DAT"`, which printed `wrote 609 bytes` on FkLua master b88965d, the same 609 bytes the retired encoder wrote and the same bytes `testdata/ingame/flipped.golden.dat` has carried since the customizer round.

**What survives is the pin, and it moved from a Go suite into the gate.** A byte golden for somebody else's writer still says something, and it says the only thing this repository can still assert about that writer: for THIS JSON it produces THESE bytes. The gate `cmp`s the fresh write against flipped.golden.dat before the first engine run and refuses on a difference, printing both sizes and naming the two causes (the codec upstream changed, or flipped.json was edited without re-recording) and the remedy, `scripts/run-ingame.sh --update`, which now re-records the dat as well as the two hash rows and does it early, because that file is a function of flipped.json and of the writer and of nothing the engine does. The refusal is a `refuse()` rather than a `fail()` on purpose: the mismatch is in the run's own INPUT, so continuing would spend six engine runs producing a page of failures about a settings file nobody pinned. An fklua too old to have the subcommand is a separate verdict and is detected before the write, by the one string that means it, `unknown command "modsettings"` on the dispatch miss; it exits NOT RUN naming what FKLUA_CHECKOUT points at and the remedy, an FkLua checkout at c21ff07 or later. Three red proofs, each observed and reverted: one byte appended to the golden refused at the cmp with `written 609, golden 610` and exit 1 with no engine reached; `git -C ../FkLua archive c21ff07~1` (7cc0d25, which still carries scripts/lib-engine.sh) unpacked into a scratch checkout produced the NOT RUN and exit 1, also with no engine reached; and flipped.json's `tips-count` edited from 40 to 41 refused at the same cmp with `written 609, golden 609`, which is the case a size check alone would miss.

**And a reader buys an assertion the gate could not make before.** `fklua modsettings read` means the file the ENGINE REWROTE after a flipped run can be read back rather than assumed, which is FkLua's own assertion 6 (`scripts/run-datastage.sh`): the engine resets a number out of its range and a dropdown value off its list to the default, so a row it discarded is still sitting in the file this script wrote and only the file the engine wrote can say so. `dump_once` now keeps `$moddir/mod-settings.dat` per row as `settled-<lang>-<run>.dat`, and one jq query over `fklua modsettings read` of the rust flipped row reports every key of flipped.json's `startup` the rewrite does not agree with. It is empty on 2.0.77: all eight installed values survive, in a rewrite that carries all seventeen settings the mod declares. The query is not vacuous, measured by doctoring a copy of that JSON (tips-count 40 to 41, chain-links renamed away) and re-running it, which printed `fkrecipes-example-tips-count: installed 40, settled 41; fkrecipes-example-chain-links: installed "long", settled null`. Both dump-hash rows for 2.0.77 are unchanged by the whole step (`run-ingame: OK`, 35 seconds wall, twelve and eleven seconds of engine per language, mod set base, core, elevated-rails, quality, space-age), which is what makes the deletion a substitution rather than a change.

**The adversarial review of that step, and what it closed.** Two of its five findings were defects in the harness rather than in its prose. The first: the refusal for a missing `testdata/ingame/flipped.golden.dat` ran unconditionally, in front of the `if [ "$UPDATE" = 1 ]` block, so the remedy it named was the command that had just refused and `--update` could not bootstrap a golden that was never recorded or had gone missing. Measured with the golden moved aside: `scripts/run-ingame.sh --update` printed `run-ingame: no byte golden at .../flipped.golden.dat  Record it deliberately with: scripts/run-ingame.sh --update`, exit 1, file still absent. The check now sits behind `[ "$UPDATE" != 1 ] && [ ! -f "$FLIPPED_GOLDEN_DAT" ]`, and the capture needs nothing there to work: `cmp -s FILE MISSING` exits 2 with zero bytes on stdout and zero on stderr (BSD cmp, measured here), so the `else` arm records it. Both directions red-proven: with the golden moved aside `--update` printed `== byte golden recorded: ... (609 bytes)` and finished `run-ingame: OK` in 34 seconds, and the bytes it recorded are byte for byte the ones moved aside (`cmp`, exit 0, sha256 `a7398e07...`), with `dump-sha256.txt` byte for byte too; moved aside without `--update` the gate still refuses, exit 1, no engine run, and now its remedy works. The second: the read-back's jq query passed vacuously on an empty document, because jq over zero input runs its program zero times, so `DRIFT` came back empty and the ok line printed. Red-proven by folding `: >"$SETTLED_JSON"` into the read, which succeeds and leaves 0 bytes behind: without the guard the gate printed `ok: the engine's own rewrite still carries every value the flipped file installed` and exited 0; with `[ ! -s "$SETTLED_JSON" ]` in front of the query it printed `FAIL: the engine's rewrite read back as an empty document` and `run-ingame: FAILED`, exit 1. The same break under `--update` printed `refusing to record the hash rows from a failing run` and left `dump-sha256.txt` untouched (`cmp`, exit 0).

**And three wordings the review was right about.** Two capture refusals said "a golden" where only the hash rows are meant, which is wrong twice over: `flipped.golden.dat` has already been re-recorded by the time either one prints, deliberately, and it is not keyed by a mod set at all. Both now say "the hash rows", and the `--strict` header says in one clause that the byte golden is a function of flipped.json and of the writer alone and so has nothing for strict to guard. The byte-mismatch message named two causes and the red proof that produced it used a third, so it now names that one too, a golden edited or corrupted in the tree, and says it is the cause that moves no dump hash: re-observed by appending one byte to the golden, `written 609 bytes` against `golden 610 bytes`, exit 1 before any engine run. CLAUDE.md's gate row was silent on both the FkLua checkout the gate now needs (c21ff07 or later via FKLUA_CHECKOUT, NOT RUN without it) and the read-back of the engine's own rewrite; it names both now. After the fixes the gate is `run-ingame: OK` in 35 seconds, thirteen and eleven seconds of engine per language, `wrote 609 bytes`, both rows matching the unmoved golden for Factorio 2.0.77.

**Reading the jumps row (follow-up item 6, the read commit).** Both gate scripts now read `.jumps.data` out of `fklua mod --report` through one function, `report_jumps LANG REPORT` in scripts/lib-report.sh (one copy, sourced by both, because two gates packaging the same two guests must say the same thing about the same number), and print one line per language: the widest span BEFORE the relay with its percentage of the limit and its function, whether the relay carried it and through how many stations, and the module's widest block with its function and the block room left over it. The line refuses on exactly one value, a negative block room (the relay having nowhere to put a station, naming the function, the run, the hop and FkLua's docs/lua-limits.md), and prints NOT RUN naming the remedy (an FkLua checkout at 2a541a7 or later via FKLUA_CHECKOUT) when the report has no `jumps` object, which is what an older fklua writes; run-ingame.sh now packages with `--report` and reads the row on each language's first run, before that language's first engine run. From `scripts/run-mirror.sh` at this commit, FkLua at b88965d, exit 0, and identically from `scripts/run-ingame.sh`:

```
   go data jumps: widest span 988326 bytes (151% of the 655355-byte limit) in (*github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go.Lib).PlanData, relayed through 12 stations; widest block 13029 bytes in github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go.copyValue, 314648 bytes of block room
   rust data jumps: widest span 545785 bytes (83% of the 655355-byte limit) in _RNvMNtCsg99gZ7im5TJ_9fkrecipes4dataNtNtB4_4plan3Lib9plan_data, no relay needed; widest block 3800 bytes in _RNvNtCsg99gZ7im5TJ_9fkrecipes5value8not_text, 323877 bytes of block room
```

The whole objects, `jq -c '.jumps.data' tmp/mirror/report-go.json` and the same for rust after that run:

```json
{"limit_bytes":655355,"hop_bytes":327677,"widest_span_bytes":988326,"widest_span_function":"(*github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go.Lib).PlanData","stations":12,"widest_span_after_relay_bytes":328114,"widest_block_bytes":13029,"widest_block_function":"github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go.copyValue","block_room_bytes":314648,"relayed":true,"relayed_functions":[{"function":"(*github.com/Techrocket9/fkrecipes/go.Lib).PlanData","widest_span_bytes":988326,"stations":12,"widest_span_after_relay_bytes":328114,"widest_block_bytes":802,"block_room_bytes":326875}]}
{"limit_bytes":655355,"hop_bytes":327677,"widest_span_bytes":545785,"widest_span_function":"_RNvMNtCsg99gZ7im5TJ_9fkrecipes4dataNtNtB4_4plan3Lib9plan_data","stations":0,"widest_span_after_relay_bytes":545785,"widest_block_bytes":3800,"widest_block_function":"_RNvNtCsg99gZ7im5TJ_9fkrecipes5value8not_text","block_room_bytes":323877,"relayed":false,"relayed_functions":[]}
```

**The block this file recorded was one function's, not the module's.** The r5 table above records widest block 802 and block room 326,875 for the Go guest; both numbers are still in the object, in `relayed_functions[0]`, and the module's own figures are 13,029 bytes in `copyValue` against 314,648 bytes of room. The Rust row moved the same way with no relay in it at all: 461 and 327,216 become 3,800 in `value::not_text` against 323,877. That is b88965d's change arriving on the guests it was written about: the ceiling is set by the worst run anywhere in the module, not by the worst run inside the widest span, and on both guests it sits in a helper the optimizer did not inline rather than in the function the span names (`not_text` is this pass's own hex-rendering helper). Recorded the old way, the Go room was overstated by 12,227 bytes and the Rust room by 3,339, the same shape of error as reading the post-relay span as headroom.

**The Go guest grew and the Rust guest shrank since the figures already in this file.** The r5 table reads 954,969 bytes of span (146 percent) over 11 stations for Go and 559,026 (85 percent) with no relay for Rust; the seam paragraph's later reading of the same report at 3601004 is 962,558 (11 stations) and 561,174. This head reads 988,326 (151 percent) over 12 stations and 545,785 (83 percent), still with no relay, so the five commits since 3601004 (ef85503, b2e47b6, da11cf5, 947ce44, bf95d92) put 25,768 bytes into the Go module's widest jump and took 15,389 out of the Rust one's. The Rust half has 109,570 bytes of span to spare before the relay has to run at all and 323,877 bytes of block room after that; the Go half is half again over the limit and loads only because the relay carries it, with 314,648 bytes of block room left. Neither number is near a wall today, and block room is the one to watch: it is what says whether the relay could still carry the module tomorrow. The Rust function names arrive as mangled v0 symbols where the Go ones are readable (`_RNvMNtCsg99gZ7im5TJ_9fkrecipes4dataNtNtB4_4plan3Lib9plan_data` is `<fkrecipes::plan::Lib>::plan_data` with its impl block in module `data`, `_RNvNtCsg99gZ7im5TJ_9fkrecipes5value8not_text` is `fkrecipes::value::not_text`; read by hand, no demangler on this machine); that is fklua's data rather than this repository's rendering and is upstream's to change if anybody minds.

**Sizes at the same head, for the record.** `wc -c tmp/mirror/datastage-go.wasm tmp/mirror/datastage-rust.wasm` gives 1,019,763 and 232,977 bytes (the Go figure three quarters DWARF, as part one measured). The packaged modules, `wc -l` and `wc -c` over `tmp/mirror/mods-{go,rust}/fkrecipes-example_0.1.0/fk_data_module.lua`, are 95,017 lines and 3,647,117 bytes for Go against 79,515 and 3,177,137 for Rust. The report's own `outputs.data_lua_bytes` reads 3,646,858 and 3,176,878, exactly 259 bytes under each file on disk, and the 259 are the factory wrapper: `outputs.data_lua_bytes` is the unwrapped chunk (`len(dsrc)` in cmd/fklua/main.go) and the file on disk is `wrapDataChunk` of it (internal/factorio/mod.go), a fixed 255-byte header ending in `return function(...)` plus `end` and a newline, which is why `DataChunkLineOffset` exists there and why both languages show the same difference.

**The review of the reader, and what it closed.** The refusal for a negative block room said the module is refused "the day its widest span crosses" the limit, and on both guests the widest span belongs to a different function from the block and has already crossed it; it now says the day the span in the block's own function crosses (FkLua's own wording), and says the widest span above may belong to another function. The status of the one jq call is read into a variable declared on its own line, because `local row="$(jq ...)"` is local's status; the reviewer found that none of the three red proofs exercised that guard (non-JSON and empty reports are both caught by the separate empty-row check), and supplied the input that does: a valid report with a truncated second document appended, over which jq prints the row and then fails. Observed at this commit with a scratch driver: the shipped file refuses (`jq: parse error: Unfinished JSON term at EOF at line 102`, then `the packaging report at trunc.json is not readable JSON`, exit 1), and a scratch copy with `local` in front of the assignment prints the full jump line and exits 0 over the same file. An empty function name would have collapsed under `IFS=$'\t' read` (tab is IFS whitespace) and shifted every later field left, so the two names now fall back to `(no function)` in the jq filter, measured to print as such; jq is checked as the function's own precondition beside `refuse()`; the line says "relayed through N stations" or "no relay needed" so a 151 percent guest reads as green for a stated reason; run-ingame.sh's modsettings remedy names 2a541a7 as the floor (a reader who followed the c21ff07 remedy would have cleared one NOT RUN and landed on the next); the mirror's gate row names jq among its needs. Red proofs at the read commit, each observed: an fklua without the object (`git -C /path/to/FkLua archive 2a541a7~1`, which is c21ff07, with the modsettings subcommand and no occurrence of "jumps" in cmd/fklua) makes both scripts print `NOT RUN: the packaging report for go carries no jumps object ... Point FKLUA_CHECKOUT at an FkLua checkout at 2a541a7 or later` and exit 1, run-ingame.sh with no dump log written, so before any engine run; `block_room_bytes` set to -1 in a copy of the real report refuses with the sentence above and exit 1 where the real report exits 0 with the line printed; a report that is not JSON, an empty one and a missing one each refuse naming the file. The percentage is rounded rather than truncated so it agrees with the one `fklua mod` prints (151, not 150).

**Gates at the read commit**, exit codes read directly: `bash -n` and shellcheck 0.11.0 over the three scripts 0; `cd go && gofmt -l .` prints nothing (0), `go vet ./...` 0, `go test ./...` 0, `go test -race ./...` 0, `cd go/examples/notext && go vet .` 0, the example's tinygo build 0; `cd rust && cargo fmt --check` 0, `cargo test` 0, `RUSTFLAGS=-Dwarnings cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets` 0, the wasm workspace build 0; `scripts/run-mirror.sh` prints the two jump rows and run-mirror: OK (0) with the transcript golden unmoved; `scripts/run-ingame.sh` prints them once per language before the engine and run-ingame: OK (0) on Factorio 2.0.77 (build 84539, re-asked), both rows matching the golden, 33 seconds wall; the repository dash grep prints nothing.

**The FkLua asks ledger, as of this pass.** Every ask this repository raised upstream, where it was raised, and what closed it:

| Ask | Raised here | Upstream | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Strings cross fkdata byte-exact in both guest libraries (the Rust side decoded lossily) | the customizer design review, decision-table row on invalid UTF-8 | b647414 (bytes in both guest libraries), 9591650 (`P::B`, a name from `keys()` can be reached) | RESOLVED upstream, adopted in the byte-string commit of this pass |
| A CLI that writes and reads `mod-settings.dat`, so a gate can put a player's value in front of the settings stage | the customizer round's harness, which built `go/internal/modsettings` because none existed | c21ff07 (`fklua modsettings write` and `read`), f80f61b (write refuses a document JSON cannot carry) | RESOLVED upstream, adopted in the retire commit of this pass |
| `--report` prints the pre-relay span and the station count, the headroom figure a library can steer by | "The pilot's second round, folded in", above | 2a541a7 (the `jumps` object: widest span, stations, span after the relay, widest block, block room), b88965d (the module row's block is the module's worst, the function named when it is not the span's) | RESOLVED upstream, adopted in the jumps commit of this pass |
| The relay's precondition stated in the data-stage documentation | the same paragraph | FkLua docs/lua-limits.md ("That shape is a block, not a distance", the block room and what a refusal quotes) and docs/data-stage.md's paragraph on a data guest that is getting large | RESOLVED upstream |
| The size trajectory: the number that says whether the modules stay loadable, measured and printed | "What this round leaves open on this side", above | the luagen size round (58bc28a, 6310005, cf08bd8, a2149b6; 37ede52 re-takes FkLua's own size table), measured in part one as about ten percent off the Go module and the Rust module out of the relay, and the `jumps` object above | RESOLVED upstream |

Carried on this side, unchanged from part one: `CostChoice.Sources` entries are not validated (an empty rung composes `{"technology-name."}`), and the localised nesting rule outruns the engine's 19-table ceiling past about 343 presets in either kind of dropdown. **BOTH CLAUSES ARE SUPERSEDED and are left here as the record of what this pass carried forward.** The empty rung composes `{"?", {"technology-name."}, ""}` since fix round 2's decision D, and an empty raw fallback renders empty rather than a marker, which is a better outcome than this line describes and still not a validated one. The ceiling is not 19 tables: it is 20 LEVELS of depth and 20 parameters per table, with no global table budget, which fix round 2 re-measured out of FkLua's own data-stage probe; the preset ceiling that follows from it is 342, and 323 once every reference carries a wrapper. Neither is an FkLua ask. NOTHING REMAINS OPEN ON FkLua'S SIDE AS OF THIS PASS, and every commit of the pass left both goldens where it found them.

## Fix round 1: the library half of the pilot's migration assessment

An adversarial assessment of BetterBeltBalancer's update path, run on a real CLIENT for the first time (BBB `agents/migration-assessment.md`, its head `ca9aa36`, over FkRecipes 9754f49 on FkLua b88965d), numbers TWELVE findings: 1 to 11, and a twelfth that is a CLEAN row rather than a defect. Its closing section lists five recommendation items for this library, (a) to (e), and they do not sit one per finding: (a) is finding 2, (d) is finding 9, and (b), (c) and (e) are all finding 10. The round took all five and went past them, which is why the sections below are headed by findings rather than by letters: they cover findings 2, 1, 10 (in two parts) and 5, 6 and 9 together. Finding 1 is in there because its own recommendation, (f), was addressed to the consumer and the library turned out to own the mechanism. What follows is what each one now does, the measurement behind it, what moved, and what the consumer must do to adopt it. Four commits, each with its own adversarial review before it landed; every review found something, and "What the reviews found" below says what.

### Finding 2, BLOCKED, and it is the round's centre: a refused text was a lock-out

The assessment reached the client and established that a refused startup text CANNOT be corrected from inside the game. On 2.0.77 (build 84539, mac-arm64, steam): the engine rewrites `mod-settings.dat` on every successful load and on NO failed one, so nothing in a failed run edits the file that caused it (three consecutive runs, one sha256); the `Error loading mods` dialog offers `Disable listed mods`, `Disable all mods`, `Manage mods`, `Restart`, `Exit` and a `Reset mod settings` checkbox, and walking `Manage mods` shows a Mods screen with no Mod settings button whose `Back` returns to the same dialog; disabling and re-enabling does not drop the value and a disabled mod's settings do not appear on the settings screen at all; the one measured escape costs six steps, every startup preference in the file and the mod being disabled.

**The rule now.** A value the PLAYER types never introduces a refusal a player who typed nothing would not also have hit; an input the AUTHOR declares still refuses. SEVEN kinds of value fall back, and the enumeration is the whole count: both text settings' parse arms and their not-text arms (4), the research count, the research seconds, and a bound crafting time. They are not seven code sites in either half, and the two halves do not agree on the number, because each shares its helpers differently: Go reaches them through four `noteFallback` calls in three functions (`resolveTextList` carries both arms and is called for both text settings, `costNumber` is called for the count and for the seconds, `craftTimeNumber` for the time), Rust through five `note_fallback` calls in five (`read_text_setting` shares the not-text arm between the two text settings, while `resolve_text_ingredients`, `resolve_custom_cost`, `read_cost_number` and the crafting-time arm of `resolve` carry the rest). The line, once per setting however many declarations read it (its outcome clause is as THIS round wrote it; fix round 3's decision 2 reworded it to `The mod loaded as though that <field> had been left alone`, and the same rewording applies to the engine transcript quoted further down):

```
fkrecipes: ERROR: fkrecipes-example-rivet-ingredients, entry 2 ("2 iron-stik"): no item or fluid is named iron-stik. The mod loaded with its own default instead; fix the text under Settings > Mod settings > Startup, then restart.
```

`ERROR` is uppercase because Factorio's `log()` has one channel and no severity (verified: `fk_log` maps to the global `log`, there is no second channel and no level parameter), so the severity has to be in the text; uppercase also keeps it out of a case-sensitive grep for the engine's own `Error` lines while a case-insensitive one still finds it. The reason is the sentence the refusal would have carried with the constant prefix trimmed, and six setting-layer sentences moved into named producers so the trim is total over a closed set.

**THE FALLBACK IS NOT TOTAL, and the review is what established that.** A text that falls back lands on the author's declaration, and the author's declaration can still refuse: a modpack that removes every science pack a technology declares, or a ladder collapsing above the item ceiling. Measured in both halves: a player who typed a working list into such a modpack loads, and one typo later refuses. The property that IS provable is the narrow one above, and it is now stated that way in all eight places that had carried an absolute one. What the round can still do for that player is a partial answer to the SECOND HALF of the assessment's item (a), which offered two alternatives: "either a fallback with a loud log for the text settings, or a refusal message that tells the player the one thing that works, which is to move the dropdown off Custom". The round took the first outright. For the case the first cannot reach it also does something about the second, though not the thing (a) names: because no log op reaches the host on a refused load, every refusal raised after resolution carries one added sentence when a stored value fell back, naming the first such setting in walk order and pointing at the Startup tab. It does NOT name moving a dropdown off Custom, which is (a)'s own suggestion and would be advice about a different mechanism.

```
. The stored value of fkrecipes-example-chain-packs could not be used, so the mod's own declaration applied; correcting it under Settings > Mod settings > Startup is what a player can change here.
```

**The engine proof, which is what the decision rests on.** The gate's flipped row was changed to store a refused PACK text, and it is the row's ONLY changed value that the language refuses: `testdata/ingame/flipped.json` gives `fkrecipes-example-tips-packs` the text `2 automation-science-pack, 1 militar-science-pack`. So the sentence at stake is the pack one, not the recipe one, and the whole line `scripts/run-ingame.sh:666` asserts is:

```
fkrecipes: ERROR: fkrecipes-example-tips-packs, entry 2 ("1 militar-science-pack"): no science pack is named militar-science-pack. The mod loaded with its own default instead; fix the text under Settings > Mod settings > Startup, then restart.
```

Against the old code that same row exited 1 with no dump written, on the language's own refusal with the ERROR wrapper and the trailing advice absent, `fkrecipes: fkrecipes-example-tips-packs, entry 2 ("1 militar-science-pack"): no science pack is named militar-science-pack`, inside whatever load-failure wrapper the host puts round it. Against the new code: `EXIT=0`, and both dump logs carry the ERROR line. Those exit codes and dumps are the engine gate's own, from `scripts/run-ingame.sh` on Factorio 2.0.77 (build 84539); nothing in the repository reproduces them without a binary, and the pinned sentence is what a reader without one can check. The script also asserts there is EXACTLY ONE `fkrecipes: ERROR: ` line, because an assertion that only checked for presence could not tell one from three.

Still refusing, each with its reason recorded: every author declaration, including the round trip that renders and reparses a text setting's declared default; and the unoffered stored dropdown value, because the engine resets an out-of-list value before any stage runs, so the library never sees one through the game.

### Finding 1, BLOCKED: a ladder that landed twice on one name emitted a list the engine refuses

BBB's ladders all end on the constant `iron-plate`, so its vanilla preset on a mod set without `transport-belt` resolved the third rung onto `iron-plate` and the engine refused the whole load with `Duplicate item ingredients are not allowed (iron-plate exists 2 or more times)`, exit 1, no dump, nothing naming the setting or the missing item, on a player who never opened the Startup tab.

A ladder that collapses now MERGES into the first occurrence, so declaration order survives, and item and fluid stay distinct identities:

```
fkrecipes: <recipe>: iron-plate is in the list twice after the fallbacks, so the amounts are added: 4 plus 2 is 6
fkrecipes: <recipe>: iron-plate is in the list twice after the fallbacks, and 40000 plus 30000 is above the item ceiling of 65535
```

The fluid twin names the collided rung instead of the two amounts, and that is forced rather than chosen: rendering a fluid amount means the language's own formatter, and both halves carry a source property that refuses a call to it from outside the two text-setting constructors, because such a call links the ingredient language into every consumer that declares no text setting. Injecting the call was red-proven; the observed line is quoted here with its tail elided, `data.go:1668:6: names formatListAmount, which links the ingredient language into every consumer; ...`, and the whole sentence is `go/source_test.go:336`'s format string, `"%s: names %s, which links the ingredient language into every consumer; only %s may name it"`, the last parameter being the places the guard allows. Measured alternatives, so a later change does not reach for one by reflex: Go's `FormatFloat(5e300,'g',-1,64)` is `5e+300`, Rust's `{}` is a 5 and 300 zeros, its `{:e}` is `5e300`. No pair agrees. (All three re-taken here: `strconv.FormatFloat` and a two-line `rustc` program on this machine.)

An AUTHOR's literal duplicate refuses rather than merging, which the review caught: the first shape swallowed a declared preset `[4 iron-plate, 2 iron-plate]`, composed a description reading `: 4 iron-plate, 2 iron-plate` that the player's own field refuses, and emitted `6`. The engine used to refuse that loudly. Now it refuses at plan time, in BOTH SHAPES, because a pack list is not an ingredient list and does not borrow its sentence: `fkrecipes: the recipe <name> names <name> twice; each ingredient is taken once` for a recipe's own list or a dropdown preset (`go/data.go:1363`, `rust/src/data.rs:1963`, reached from the recipe spec, from each choice preset in the data planner, and from the same presets again in the settings planner), and `fkrecipes: the technology <name> names <pack> twice; each science pack is taken once` for a unit's packs (`go/data.go:1379`, `rust/src/data.rs:2135`). Between them that is every place either can be declared. With that in, the merge is reachable only from a ladder collapse, which is what makes the log line's phrase "after the fallbacks" true wherever it appears.

**Measured, because the first shape argued it instead.** A research unit ingredient really is held in 16 bits. Probed with a Lua-only mod under `--dump-data`, `write-data` pointed at a private directory, on 2.0.77 (build 84539):

| unit ingredient amount | result |
|---|---|
| 65535 | exit 0, dumped as `[["automation-science-pack",65535]]` |
| 65536 | exit 1 |
| 2147483648 | exit 1 |
| 9007199254740992 | exit 1 |

`Error while loading technology prototype "packceiling-probe" (technology): Value (65536) outside of range. The data type allows values from 0 to 65535 in property tree at ROOT.technology.packceiling-probe.unit.ingredients[0][1]`

So the merged-pack ceiling of 65535 is right, and the declared pack path that capped at `maxExactInt` was emitting a load failure. It refuses by name now: `fkrecipes: the technology <name> takes 70000 of automation-science-pack, and a science pack amount goes up to 65535`.

### Finding 10, AWKWARD: three invisible characters, three policies, and a refusal quoting text the player cannot see

`U+00AD` was named by its code point, `U+FEFF`, `U+200B`, `U+200C` and `U+200D` (`listStripped` at 9754f49, exactly those four) were silently DELETED anywhere in the text so `2 iron<ZWSP>plate` refused with `no item or fluid is named ironplate` (a word not on the player's screen and unfindable by searching for it), and, on the assessment's own measurement, a NUL survived a load and cut a refusal message in half. What this repository had measured about a NUL said the opposite; the paragraph below is where that was settled, and both notes turned out to be right about their own range.

One policy now, and the wider one. The strip is gone: those four points were already in the invisible set, so deleting `stripInvisible` and `is_stripped` is the whole of it. The set grew from 11 ranges and 114 code points to 28 and 4287, both counted off the range tables themselves (`git show 9754f49:go/ingredientlist.go` lines 1173-1193 for the old one, `go/ingredientlist.go`'s `isInvisibleRune` for the new one, each expanded and counted with Python's `unicodedata` at Unicode 16.0.0). It is DERIVED rather than guessed, and it is THREE PARTS that account for the whole rather than a list with a remainder:

- every `Cf` code point in Unicode, all 170 (Python's 16.0.0 table and Go's 15.0.0 table agree on all 170, re-checked here in both);
- every code point carrying the `Default_Ignorable` derivation, 4174, all of them inside the predicate;
- the characters that show as blank but are neither, which is the C0 and C1 controls plus the spaces the derivation subtracts as whitespace: `U+2000..U+200A`, `U+2028`, `U+2029`, `U+202F`, `U+205F` and `U+3000`.

Those three ARE the whole: `Cf` union `Default_Ignorable` is 4206, the third part adds exactly the remaining 81, and 4206 plus 81 is 4287. `U+FFF0..U+FFF8` is not a fourth part and never was one: it is inside `Default_Ignorable` already, so listing it as a "plus" double-counted it. Private-use points stay out, because a player's font can give one a glyph; unassigned points are in only where the standard reserves the run as default-ignorable, and that is THREE RUNS rather than one block: `U+2065`, `U+FFF0..U+FFF8`, and most of the plane-14 tag block. The tag-block range contributes 3759 of the 3769 unassigned points the predicate takes and those other two runs the remaining 10; 3769 is the total, which is what the source comment and `agents/customizer-design.md` both say, and every one of them carries `Other_Default_Ignorable_Code_Point`. The predicate is still a closed-range match with no Unicode table linked, because size is a measured property here. The whitespace set is untouched and still folds to a space, which is what keeps its SIX members that are also in the invisible set separating words rather than refusing: tab, LF, CR, `U+2007`, `U+202F` and `U+3000`. The whitespace question runs first.

Second, `quotable()` in each half: every message that puts a piece of the player's text between quotation marks renders each invisible-set member as `U+XXXX`, the same token the "an invisible character (U+XXXX)" clause prints. The claim worth making is that one, not a site count, because the halves do not agree on the count and never could: Go calls it at 11 sites in 6 functions (`entryProblem`, `parseEntry`, `noNameProblem`, `manyNamesProblem`, `classifyPiece`, `strangeCharacterProblem`) and Rust at 10 in 5 (`parse`, `one_entry`, `no_name`, `two_names`, `classify_plain`), because the two halves group their message builders differently. What holds the property is `testdata/ingredient-list/cases.txt`, which carries a case per range whose refusal spells the token and which both suites run byte for byte, plus the per-member predicate test in each half. NO TEST SCANS GENERATED MESSAGES for an unescaped member, and no such scan is reproducible from this repository; an earlier draft of this section reported one over 8,896,512 messages, and that figure came from a reviewer's own differential probe over the two halves rather than from anything committed here.

**The NUL, and TWO MEASUREMENTS THAT DISAGREED.** Two notes describe the same engine, both say measured, and they read as contradictory. BOTH NOTES TURNED OUT TO BE RIGHT ABOUT THEIR OWN RANGE, on opposite sides of a boundary at 22 bytes, and each stated an unqualified universal that is false over most of the other side; fix round 1b measured it, and its own subsection below carries the instrument, the six arms and the two sweeps.

- BBB's `agents/migration-assessment.md`, finding 10, on 2.0.77: a NUL in a stored setting value "survives a successful load and the engine's rewrite byte for byte", and on a refusal it cuts the message in half, ending at `entry 1 ("2 iron-plate` with no reason given. That assessment could not type into the settings field at all (secure input was active for its whole session), so every value it tested was installed by file with `fklua modsettings write`; the finding does not say which of its two arms, headless or client, this row came from. IT IS TRUE OF 23 BYTES AND UP, and the length is an INFERENCE rather than something the assessment recorded: finding 10 gives the cut refusal and says nothing at all about the stored value or its length, so the value is read back off the `2 iron-plate` its message got out before the cut, and 23 and up is where whatever it tested must have sat, because that is the only range in which both halves of its sentence hold. Arm E reproduces both halves at 29 bytes.
- This repository's `agents/customizer-design.md`, in the customizer round's probe list, also on 2.0.77: a NUL byte truncates the stored value inside the engine and the truncation is persisted. That row records neither how the byte was installed nor where the truncation was read back. IT IS TRUE OF 22 BYTES AND DOWN, which is where whatever it tested must have sat, because that is the only range in which the sentence holds; the inference it drew, that a NUL therefore never reaches the guest at all, is false for every longer value and it has been deleted from that row.

THE ANSWER IS LENGTH, AND THE TWO RUNS DIFFER IN THE LENGTH OF THE VALUE THEY STORED, not in how the byte was installed: a stored value of 22 bytes or fewer is cut at its NUL between the file and the guest and a load that COMPLETES persists the cut, while 23 to 48 arrives whole and is rewritten byte for byte (48 is where the sweep stopped, and every value in it carried exactly one NUL, so "first NUL" is untested). What this round's decisions rested on is CONFIRMED rather than assumed now: the engine does cut a load-failure message at a NUL, C-string fashion, and the escaping is what makes every message THE INGREDIENT LANGUAGE composes independent of that, because none of them can carry the byte in the first place. ONE PRODUCER OUTSIDE THE LANGUAGE IS NOT COVERED and is not fixed here: `readDropdown` (`go/data.go:1575`) and `read_dropdown` (`rust/src/data.rs:2316-2319`) put a stored dropdown value into `holds "..."` with no escaper, and that sentence is a load-failure message. The measurement, both sweeps, the confirming run and that open item are in fix round 1b's NUL subsection below.

The 2000-character ceiling's stated reason went stale with the escaping and now carries numbers instead of an adjective: the worst-case message is 14,129 bytes for 2000 `U+E0001` and 12,128 for 2000 `U+200B`. Both re-derived here from the message producers and a setting named `mymod-parts`, the corpus's own: 2000 seven-character tokens plus a 129-byte frame, and 2000 six-character ones plus a 128-byte frame. The "it was 8,162 and 180" beside them is about code this round deleted and was not re-taken. No quoted entry is truncated, because finding 2's change moves that text out of the load-failure dialog and into the log.

### Finding 10, second half: the reserved words match the way a player types them

`"  default  "` was accepted and `DEFAULT` refused, and because a refused text repeats for ever it stayed refused. `default` and `none` now fold on ASCII case at three sites: the whole text, the entry, and the renderer's tag decision. The third is what keeps render-then-parse an identity, and `Default`, `None` and `defaults` joined both identity fixtures so the claim has a witness rather than a comment. ONLY THE GO HALF WRITES THE FOLD OUT, and the reason is the shape of the two standard libraries rather than a symmetry: `strings.EqualFold` is whole-Unicode simple folding, which is the WRONG rule here (it reads the Kelvin sign U+212A as a `k`), so `isReservedWord` is a byte loop; Rust's `eq_ignore_ascii_case` IS the rule and is called at all three sites. Go was written by hand to match Rust, not both halves written out to avoid both libraries. What makes it worth writing down at all is that neither of today's two words carries a letter the two functions disagree about, so an inherited fold would look correct until a third word landed and nothing would say so.

### Findings 5, 6 and 9, MISLED: the composed description said none of what a player needed

The composed tooltip printed each preset twice in two vocabularies separated only by `": "`, the client truncates the closed dropdown's label at roughly 37 characters so the half that works is the half a player never sees, the 2000-character limit is the library's and no prototype key declares it, and nothing said what happens when a text cannot be used.

A text setting's description gains two lines after `"\ndefault: <rendered list>"`, the second of them as this round wrote it; fix round 3's decision 2 reworded its middle clause to `and the field behaves as though it said default`:

```
\nWrite internal names, as the default line above does, in at most 2000 characters.
\nA text this mod cannot use is set aside and that default applies instead; the reason is in the log, or in the load error if the load stops anyway.
```

The 2000 comes from `maxListChars` / `MAX_TEXT`, held by a source property that forbids the ceiling's own decimal rendering in any message literal, because a typed `2000` and the expression render the same bytes while the ceiling happens to be 2000. The second sentence went through the review: its first shape promised "the mod loads with that default instead, and the log says why", which is exactly false in the one case finding 2 leaves open, and where `withFallbackNote` existed precisely because the log is not reached (fix round 2's decision 2 renamed that composer `fallbackFact` / `fallback_fact` and cut the screen route out of its sentence; the load error is still the only place the reason can be, and that composer is still what puts it there).

An INGREDIENT preset line's `": "` became `"\n  type: "`. A COST preset line is unchanged: a localised technology name after a localised label is one vocabulary, not two.

**Measured in the engine's own dump** (`mod-settings-dump.json`, 2.0.77):

```
fkrecipes-example-rivet-ingredients
["",["mod-setting-description.fkrecipes-example-rivet-ingredients"],"\ndefault: 1 iron-plate","\nWrite internal names, ...","\nA text this mod cannot use ..."]

fkrecipes-example-quench-medium
["",["mod-setting-description...."],["","\n",["string-mod-setting....-water"],"\n  type: 2 tungsten-plate, 4 fkrecipes-example-steel-rivet, 1 tungsten-carbide"],["","\n",["string-mod-setting....-oil"],"\n  type: 2 steel-plate, ..."]]
```

Counting parameters the way the engine counts them (elements after the leading key, which is the convention `maxLocalisedParams = 20` uses): the text setting spends 4 of 20 at depth 2 of 20 (5 of 20 once the switch line lands, and the depth is unchanged), an ingredient dropdown 3 at the top and 3 per preset line at depth 3, a cost dropdown unchanged at 3 and 4. The ceilings were re-measured before the shape was fixed, out of FkLua's data-stage probe rows `locstring-params-20`/`-21`, `locstring-nest-19`/`-20` and `locstring-nested-groups`, which take them on a `string-setting`'s `localised_description`, the very field this writes. The grouping rule is unaffected: a preset line is one parameter of its group whatever its head says. Both halves emit byte-identical tables, checked by pulling all eight composed descriptions out of each half's own engine dump and comparing (3094 bytes each, identical).

**Why the shape is honest at 37 characters, and what is still open.** The truncation is of the dropdown's own label in the closed widget, and the label is the consumer's string; the composed description is the tooltip. Under the old shape the tooltip line was `<label>: <internal list>`, one run of text whose halves were separated only by `": "`, so nothing marked where the copyable half began and a truncated label showed only the half that refuses. Under the new shape there is no composite line left to truncate: each preset's copyable half opens its own indented line under the label. The mitigation is real and not total, measured: a player who retypes the whole line gets `entry 1 ("type: 4 iron-plate"): ":" has no place here; ...`, the same message class the old defect produced. And the premise the design rests on carries its condition: the client measured seven lines (the pilot's six presets plus the consumer's entry, on the OLD shape) as readable and unclipped. The same tooltip under the new shape is 13 lines.
